in order to adjust the kind of the previous judgment
via
K-Conv
. But to do so, we need to find a derivation
of
\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash{J_{1}\;\mathsf{kd}}
that is a strict
sub-derivation of our current instance of
K-App
.

Fortunately, this is always possible, thanks to the validity
condition
\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta,Y\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ_{1}\vdash{J_{2}\;\mathsf{kd}}
.
Since the contexts of kind formation judgments are always
well-formed themselves (see
Lemma
B.1
), it suffices to
traverse the derivation tree of this judgment upwards along kind
formation and kinding rules until one arrives at a “leaf” – an
instance of
K-Var
,
K-Top
or
K-Bot
–
which holds a well-formedness derivation for the current context.
That context formation derivation, in turn, contains a
sub-derivation of the desired kind formation judgment. Readers
who are skeptical of this somewhat informal argument are
encouraged to state and prove a helper lemma that combines the IH
with the “lookup procedure” just described. The lemma is proven
simultaneously with the main lemma, by induction on kind formation
and kinding derivations.

With all the validity conditions in place, we
apply
ST-App
to obtain

\Gamma,\Sigma\;\vdash\;({B_{1}}\,{B_{2}})[A_{2}/X]\;\leq\;({B_{1}}\,{B_{2}})[A_{1}/X]\;:\;J_{2}[A_{1}/X][B_{2}[A_{2}/X]/Y].

To complete the proof of
2
, we
use
ST-Conv
and

\displaystyle\Gamma,\Sigma

\displaystyle{}\;\vdash\;J_{2}[A_{1}/X][B_{2}[A_{2}/X]/Y]

(by
Lemma
B.4
)

\displaystyle{}\;=\;J_{2}[A_{2}/X][B_{2}[A_{2}/X]/Y]

(by
Lemma
B.6
)

\displaystyle{}\;\equiv\;J_{2}[B_{2}/Y][A_{2}/X]

(by the IH)

\displaystyle{}\;=\;J_{2}[B_{2}/Y][A_{1}/X]

using our earlier result

\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash J_{1}[A_{2}/X]=J_{1}[A_{1}/X]

in the first step and the final validity condition
of
K-App
in the last. ∎

We are now ready to prove
Lemma
B.11
in the extended
system, simultaneously with the following lemma.

Lemma B.15.

Subtypes inhabiting interval kinds are proper subtypes. If

\;\Gamma\vdash A\leq B:C\intv D
, then also

\Gamma\vdash A\leq B:{*}
.

Proof of
Lemma
B.11
and

Lemma
B.15
– extended version.

All the validity properties are proven simultaneously with

Lemma
B.15
, by induction on the derivations of the
respective premises. The proof is now mostly routine, thanks to the
validity conditions. The only interesting cases are those of
of
ST-App
, where we use the functionality lemma to adjust
the kind of the right-hand validity proof, and
ST-Intv
,
where we use
Lemma
B.15
. The proof of
Lemma
B.15

uses subtyping validity in turn.
∎

Corollary B.16.

Equal types in intervals are equal as proper types. If

\;\Gamma\vdash A=B:C\intv D
, then also

\Gamma\vdash A=B:{*}
.

B.3.2.
Equivalence

The next and final step in our program for
proving
Lemma
B.11
is to establish the equivalence of
the two declarative systems.

Lemma B.17.

The original and extended declarative systems are equivalent:

\Gamma\vdash_{\sf d}\!\mathcal{J}
iff
\Gamma\vdash_{\sf e}\!\mathcal{J}
.

Thanks to this equivalence, all the validity properties laid out
in
Lemma
B.11
also hold for the original judgments of
the declarative system.
Our original functionality lemma (
Lemma
B.12
) and the
following strengthened version of
Lemma
B.5
follow as
corollaries of validity and
Lemmas
B.14
and

B.5
, respectively.

Corollary B.18 (context narrowing – strong version).

(1)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash A\leq B:{*}

and
\Gamma,x\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muB,\Delta\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
, then

\Gamma,x\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muA,\Delta\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
.

(2)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash J\leq K
and

\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
, then

\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ,\Delta\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
,

B.4.
Admissible Congruence Rules for Type and Kind Equality

Thanks to the validity properties established in

§
B.3
,
we are able to prove a number of admissible
congruence rules

for kind and type equality.
These follow the same structure as the corresponding subkinding and
subtyping rules but are generally a bit simpler.
First, we no longer need to pay attention to the variance (or
polarity) of constructor arguments because equality is symmetric.
Second, the left-hand validity conditions present in the
rules
SK-DArr
and
ST-All
become redundant in the
corresponding equality rules because the kind annotations in the left-
and right-hand sides are convertible.
Finally, thanks to symmetry, only one rule is needed for

\beta
-conversion, and likewise for
\eta
-conversion.

Lemma B.19.

Kind equality is a congruence with respect to the interval and
dependent arrow kind formers, i.e. the following kind equality
rules are admissible.

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A_{1}=A_{2}:{*}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash B_{1}=B_{2}:{*}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\intv B_{1}=A_{2}\intv B_{2}\end{array}}

(
KEq-Intv
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash J_{1}=J_{2}\quad\quad\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ_{1}\vdash K_{1}=K_{2}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{1}}=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{2}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}\end{array}}

(
KEq-DArr
)

Lemma B.20.

Type equality is a congruence with respect to the various type
formers and includes
\beta
and
\eta
-conversion, i.e. the
following type equality rules are admissible.

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash K_{1}=K_{2}\quad\quad\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1}\vdash A_{1}=A_{2}:{*}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}}.\,{A_{1}}=\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{2}}.\,{A_{2}}:{*}\end{array}}

(
TEq-All
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A_{1}=A_{2}:{*}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash B_{1}=B_{2}:{*}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\rightarrow B_{1}=A_{2}\rightarrow B_{2}:{*}\end{array}}

(
TEq-Arr
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}}.\,A_{1}:({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{K}\\
\Gamma\vdash\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{2}}.\,A_{2}:({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{K}\\
\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ\vdash A_{1}=A_{2}:K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}}.\,A_{1}=\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{2}}.\,A_{2}:({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{K}\end{array}}

(
TEq-Abs
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A_{1}=A_{2}:({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{K}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash B_{1}=B_{2}:J\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{A_{1}}\,{B_{1}}={A_{2}}\,{B_{2}}:K[B_{1}/X]\end{array}}

(
TEq-App
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A_{1}=A_{2}:B\intv C\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A_{1}=A_{2}:A_{1}\intv A_{1}\end{array}}

(
TEq-Sing
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ\vdash A:K\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash B:J\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{(\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J}.\,A)}\,{B}=A[B/X]:K[B/X]\end{array}}

(
TEq-
\beta
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A:({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{K}\quad\quad X\notin\mathsf{fv}(A)\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J}.\,{A}\,{X}=A:({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{K}\end{array}}

(
TEq-
\eta
)

Proof.

The admissibility proofs of the above rules all follow the same
basic pattern.
We want to show that the left- and right-hand sides of the
conclusions are mutual subkinds or subtypes, respectively.
To do so, we employ the respective subkinding and subtyping rules,
adjusting the kinds of additional bindings and subtyping judgments
using context narrowing (
Corollary
B.18
),
subsumption
ST-Sub
, conversion
ST-Conv
and
functionality (
Lemma
3.2
) where necessary.
When additional validity properties are required,

Lemma
3.1
delivers the required well-formedness or
well-kindedness proofs.

For example, the proof of
TEq-Sing
proceeds as follows.
We note that the premise must have been derived using

ST-AntiSym
, hence we have

\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\leq A_{2}:B\intv C
and

\Gamma\vdash A_{2}\leq A_{1}:B\intv C
.
By
ST-Intv
,
Lemma
B.15
and subtyping validity

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\leq A_{2}:A_{1}\intv A_{2}\quad\text{(1a)}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\leq A_{2}:{*}\quad\text{(1b)}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash A_{1}:{*}\quad\text{(1c)}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash A_{2}\leq A_{1}:A_{2}\intv A_{1}\quad\text{(2a)}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash A_{2}\leq A_{1}:{*}\quad\text{(2b)}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash A_{2}:{*}\quad\text{(2c)}

From (1a), (1c) and (2b) we derive

\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\leq A_{2}:A_{1}\intv A_{2}

\Gamma\vdash A_{1}:{*}

(
ST-Refl
)

\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\leq A_{1}:{*}

\Gamma\vdash A_{2}\leq A_{1}:{*}

(
SK-Intv
)

\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\intv A_{2}\leq A_{1}\intv A_{1}

(
ST-Sub
)

\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\leq A_{2}:A_{1}\intv A_{1}

and similarly
\Gamma\vdash A_{2}\leq A_{1}:A_{1}\intv A_{1}
from
(2a), (2b) and (1c).
We conclude with
ST-AntiSym
.
∎

Note that the rule
TEq-Sing
(and by transitivity, the
rule
ST-Intv
) plays an important role in the proof of
subject reduction for types (
Theorem
3.3
).
It is used in the case for
K-Sing
, where it allows us to
relate
\beta
-equal types inhabiting singleton kinds.

B.5.
Admissible Rules for Higher-Order Extrema and Intervals

In this section, we state and prove admissible rules that justify the
encodings of higher-order extremal types and interval kinds given
in
§
3.1.1
of the paper (page
3.1.1
).
Many of these rules are straightforward generalizations of the
corresponding rules for the types
\top
,
\bot
and for proper type
intervals
A\intv B
.
The remaining rules and lemmas mostly deal with the family of kinds

{*}_{K}
, which plays a crucial role in the other encodings and the
proofs of their respective properties.

We start by stating and proving a formation rule for

{*}_{K}
.

Lemma B.21.

The kind
{*}_{K}
is well-formed whenever
K
is,
i.e. the following is admissible.

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{{*}_{K}\;\mathsf{kd}}\end{array}}

(Wf-KMax)

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
K
.
The base case uses
K-Bot
,
K-Top

and
Wf-Intv
to show that
{*}
is well-formed.
∎

The kind
{*}_{K}
is a widened version of
K
, i.e. the latter is
always a subkind of the former. As a consequence, any type of kind

K
is also of kind
{*}_{K}
.

Lemma B.22.

Any well-formed kind
K
is a subkind of
{*}_{K}
.

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash K\leq{*}_{K}\end{array}}

(SK-KMax)

Proof.

By straightforward induction on the structure of
K
.
∎

Corollary B.23.

If
\;\Gamma\vdash A:K
, then also
\Gamma\vdash A:{*}_{K}
.

The following two lemmas introduce admissible kinding rules for the
higher-order extremal types, and prove that
\bot_{K}
and
\top_{K}

are in fact extrema in
{*}_{K}
, i.e. they are the least and
greatest inhabitants of
{*}_{K}
, respectively.

Lemma B.24.

Higher-order extremal types are well-formed if their
index kind is.

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash\top_{K}:{*}_{K}\end{array}}

(K-TMax)

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash\bot_{K}:{*}_{K}\end{array}}
(K-TMin)

Proof.

Separately, by induction on the structure of
K
. The cases for
dependent arrow kinds use
Wf-KMax
.
∎

Lemma B.25.

The types
\top_{K}
and
\bot_{K}
are the maximal and
minimal elements of
{*}_{K}
, respectively.

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A:K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A\leq\top_{K}:{*}_{K}\end{array}}

(ST-TMax)

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A:K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash\bot_{K}\leq A:{*}_{K}\end{array}}
(ST-TMin)

Proof.

Separately, by induction on the structure of
K
.
Corollary
B.23
is
used to adjust the kind of the premises where necessary. In the
inductive step, we use
ST-Abs
and the

\eta
-rules
ST-
\eta_{1,2}
. For example, for

K=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}
we have

(by
ST-
\eta_{2}
)

\displaystyle\Gamma\;\vdash\;A\;

\displaystyle\leq\;\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}}.\,{A}\,{X}

for
X\notin\mathsf{fv}(A)

(by the IH and
ST-Abs
)

\displaystyle\leq\;\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}}.\,\top_{K_{2}}

(by definition)

\displaystyle\equiv\;\top_{({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}}

\displaystyle\;\;:\;{*}_{({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}}.

∎

Having generalized the properties of the extremal types to their
higher-order counterparts, we now turn to interval kinds. We start
with an admissible formation rule for higher-order intervals.

Lemma B.26.

Higher-order interval kinds are well-formed if their
bounds are.

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A:K\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash B:K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{A\intv_{K}B\;\mathsf{kd}}\end{array}}

(Wf-HoIntv)

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
K
. The inductive step uses
kinding validity,
K-Var
and
K-App
to expand the
bounds.
∎

The subkinding rule
SK-Intv
for proper type intervals also
generalizes straightforwardly to intervals over arbitrary type
operators.

Lemma B.27.

Higher-order interval kinds are widened in accordance
with their bounds.

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A_{2}\leq A_{1}:K\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash B_{1}\leq B_{2}:K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\intv_{K}B_{1}\leq A_{2}\intv_{K}B_{2}\end{array}}

(SK-HoIntv)

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
K
. The inductive step uses
subtyping validity,
K-Var
,
TEq-Refl

and
ST-App
to expand the bounds, and
Wf-HoIntv

to establish well-formedness of the left-hand side.
∎

Next, we would like to prove an admissible higher-order singleton
introduction rule that generalizes
K-Sing
. Ideally, we
would like to show that any well-kinded type
\Gamma\vdash A:K

inhabits its corresponding singleton kind

S(A:K)=A\intv_{K}A
.
This is not necessarily true, however.
Consider the case of an operator variable

X
with declared type
\Gamma(X)={*}\rightarrow{*}
. The
singleton kind corresponding to
X
is

S(X:{*}\rightarrow{*})=({Y}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{{*}})\rightarrow{{X}\,{Y}\!\intv{X}\,{Y}}
,
so we would like to prove that

\Gamma\vdash X:({Y}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{{*}})\rightarrow{{X}\,{Y}\!\intv{X}\,{Y}}
.
Which kinding rules could we use to adjust the kind of
X
to the
desired singleton kind? Since
K-Sing
can only be applied to
proper types, our only option is to use the subsumption
rule
K-Sub
. But unfortunately, the declared kind

{*}\rightarrow{*}
of
X
is a
strict supertype
of the
singleton kind
({Y}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{{*}})\rightarrow{{X}\,{Y}\!\intv{X}\,{Y}}
, so
this cannot work.

We can, however, assign the desired singleton kind to the

\eta
-expansion
of
X
, i.e. to

\lambda{Y}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{{*}}.\,{X}\,{Y}
. Unlike
X
, the application

{X}\,{Y}
in the body of the
\eta
-expansion is a proper type, so
we can use
K-Sing
to narrow its kind. The full derivation
is

\Gamma\vdash{{*}\;\mathsf{kd}}

\Gamma,Y\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{*}\vdash X:{*}\rightarrow{*}

\Gamma,Y\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{*}\vdash Y:{*}

(
K-App
)

\Gamma,Y\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{*}\vdash{X}\,{Y}:{*}

(
K-Sing
)

\Gamma,Y\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{*}\vdash{X}\,{Y}:{X}\,{Y}\!\intv{X}\,{Y}

(
K-Abs
)

\Gamma\vdash\lambda{Y}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{{*}}.\,{X}\,{Y}:({Y}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{{*}})\rightarrow{{X}\,{Y}\!\intv{X}\,{Y}}

This principle generalizes to arbitrary well-kinded types: the

\eta
-expansion of a well-kinded type
\Gamma\vdash A:K
always
inhabits the corresponding singleton kind
S(A:K)
.

Given a type
A
, we define the
weak
\eta
-expansion

\bar{\eta}_{K}(A)
of
A
as
\bar{\eta}_{B\intv C}(A)=A
and

\bar{\eta}_{({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{K}}(A)=\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J}.\,\bar{\eta}_{K}({A}\,{X})

where, as usual, we assume that
X\notin\mathsf{fv}(A)
. We call this
expansion “weak” because the argument
X
in the definition

\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J}.\,\bar{\eta}_{K}({A}\,{X})
of the arrow case is not

\eta
-expanded further. This means that the result is not

\eta
-long. This is sufficient for the purpose of this section; we
will define a stronger version in the next section.

As expected, a type of kind
K
is equal to its weak
\eta
-expansion
in
K
.

Lemma B.28.

Weak
\eta
-expansion is sound, i.e. if
\;\Gamma\vdash A:K
,
then
\Gamma\vdash A=\bar{\eta}_{K}(A):K
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
K
,
using
TEq-
\eta
and
TEq-Abs
in the
inductive case.
∎

Lemma B.29.

The
\eta
-expansions of type operators inhabit their higher-order
singleton intervals.

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A:K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash\bar{\eta}_{K}(A):S(A:K)\end{array}}

(K-HoSing)

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
K
. The base case follows
from
K-Sing
, the inductive step from the usual combination
of kinding validity,
K-Var
,
K-App

and
Wf-HoIntv
.
∎

Corollary B.30.

If
\Gamma\vdash B_{1}\leq A:K
and

\Gamma\vdash A\leq B_{2}:K
, then

\Gamma\vdash\bar{\eta}_{K}(A):B_{1}\intv_{K}B_{2}
.

Having found ways to form, widen and populate higher-order intervals,
we still need a way to put their bounds to use. To this end, we
introduce two
higher-order bound projection
rules, which
generalize the corresponding rules
ST-Bnd
1

and
ST-Bnd
2
for proper type intervals.

Lemma B.31 (higher-order bound projection).

Inhabitants of a higher-order interval are supertypes of its lower
bound and subtypes of its upper bound.

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A:K\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash B_{1}:K\\
\Gamma\vdash A:B_{1}\intv_{K}B_{2}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash B_{1}\leq A:K\end{array}}

(ST-HoBnd
1
)

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A:K\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash B_{2}:K\\
\Gamma\vdash A:B_{1}\intv_{K}B_{2}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A\leq B_{2}:K\end{array}}
(ST-HoBnd
2
)

These rules are a bit weaker than one might expect. In particular,
the additional premises
\Gamma\vdash A:K
,
\Gamma\vdash B_{1}:K

and
\Gamma\vdash B_{2}:K
, might seem redundant. They are necessary
because we cannot, in general, invert well-formedness judgments about
higher-order intervals. That is,
\Gamma\vdash{B_{1}\intv_{K}B_{2}\;\mathsf{kd}}

does
not
imply
\Gamma\vdash B_{1}:K
and

\Gamma\vdash B_{2}:K
, nor does

\Gamma\vdash A:B_{1}\intv_{K}B_{2}
imply
\Gamma\vdash A:K
.
To see this, consider the kind
K=\bot\intv_{\varnothing}\top
, where

\varnothing=\top\intv\bot
is the empty interval (note the absurd
bounds). The kind
K
is well-formed and inhabited by both
\bot
and

\top
, yet clearly
\bot,\top
are not inhabitants of
\varnothing
.
Note that the formation rule
Wf-HoIntv
for higher-order
intervals is not to blame: although
K
is well-formed, we cannot
prove this fact using
Wf-HoIntv
. There are simply more
well-formed higher-order intervals than can be derived
using
Wf-HoIntv
.

Proof of
Lemma
B.31
.

Separately, by induction on the structure of
K
. In the base case,
we use the interval projection
rules
ST-Bnd
1,2
as well
as
ST-Intv
and
ST-Sub
to adjust the kinds of the
resulting inequations. In the inductive step, we
use
ST-Abs
and the

\eta
-rules
ST-
\eta_{1,2}
. For example, for
the left-hand case and
K=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}
we have

(by
ST-
\eta_{2}
)

\displaystyle\Gamma\;\vdash\;B_{1}\;

\displaystyle\leq\;\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}}.\,{B_{1}}\,{X}

(by the IH and
ST-Abs
)

\displaystyle\leq\;\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}}.\,{A}\,{\,X}

(by
ST-
\eta_{1}
)

\displaystyle\leq\;A

\displaystyle\;\;:\;({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}.

∎

Thanks to the admissible kinding and subtyping rules for higher-order
intervals and extrema, we can now easily derive judgments for forming,
introducing or eliminating bounded universal quantifiers over
arbitrary type operators.

For example, well-formedness of the
higher-order universal quantifier
\forall{X\leq A}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{B}
can be
derived as

\Gamma\vdash A:K

(kinding validity)

\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}

(
K-TMin
)

\Gamma\vdash\bot_{K}:{*}_{K}

\Gamma\vdash A:K

(
Corollary
B.23
)

\Gamma\vdash A:{*}_{K}

(
Wf-HoIntv
)

\Gamma\vdash{\bot_{K}\intv_{{*}_{K}}A\;\mathsf{kd}}

(
Lemma
B.32
)

\Gamma\vdash{\bot_{K}\intv_{K}A\;\mathsf{kd}}

\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\bot_{K}\intv_{K}A\vdash B:{*}

(
K-All
)

\Gamma\vdash\forall{X\leq A}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{B}:{*}

The derivation uses the following lemma for simplifying interval
kinds; its proof is by structural induction on the index
K
.

Lemma B.32.

Let
A
,
B
be types and
K
a kind. Then

A\intv_{{*}_{K}}B\equiv A\intv_{K}B

Similar derivations exist for the introduction and elimination rules.

Corollary B.33 (bounded quantification).

The following rules for the
formation, introduction and elimination of bounded universal
quantifiers are admissible.

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A:K\quad\quad\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\bot_{K}\intv_{K}A\vdash B:{*}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash\forall{X\leq A}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{B}:{*}\end{array}}

(K-AllBnd)

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A:K\quad\quad\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\bot_{K}\intv_{K}A\vdash t:B\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash\lambda{X\leq A}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,t:\forall{X\leq A}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{B}\end{array}}
(T-TAbsBnd)

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash t:\forall{X\leq A}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{B}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash C\leq A:K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{t}\,{(\bar{\eta}_{K}(C))}:B[\bar{\eta}_{K}(C)/X]\end{array}}
(T-TAppBnd)

Similar rules for the formation, abstraction and elimination of
bounded operators are also admissible.

Note that we need to
\eta
-expand the type argument
C
in the
elimination rule
T-TAppBnd

before it can by applied to
t
. This is because
C
has kind
K
,
while the polymorphic expression
t
expects an argument of kind

\bot_{K}\intv_{K}C
. As discussed earlier,
C
is not guaranteed
to inhabit that kind but its
\eta
-expansion is –
via
K-HoSing
and a subsequent widening of its kind from

C\intv_{K}C
to
\bot_{K}\intv_{K}C
.

There is an alternative encoding of higher-order bounded
quantification (and bounded type operators) that
separates the declaration of type variables from that of the subtyping
constraints imposed by their bounds, at the cost of using an auxiliary
type variable with potentially inconsistent bounds. Assume a
partition of the set of type variable names into two distinct sets of

operator names
denoted by
X_{\sf n},Y_{\sf n},\dotsc
and

constraint names
denoted by
X_{\sf c},Y_{\sf c},\dotsc

We may then encode an upper-bounded type variable binding

X\leq A:K
as a pair of bindings
X_{\sf n}:K
,

X_{\sf c}:X_{\sf n}\intv_{K}A
, separating the declaration of the
operator name
X
from the subtyping constraint
X\leq A
. For
example, the encoding of bounded universal quantifiers according to
this scheme would be

\forall{X\leq A}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{B}=\forall{X_{\sf n}}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{\forall{X_{\sf c}}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{X_{\sf n}\intv_{K}A}.\,{B}}

where
X_{\sf c}\notin\mathsf{fv}(B)
.

The advantage of this encoding is a
cleaner separation between the uses of bounded variable bindings in
kinding and subtyping. Whenever we want to refer to the original type
variable
X
or its kind, we simply use
X_{\sf n}
. When we require a
proof of the fact that
X\leq A
we obtain one from
X_{\sf c}

via
ST-HoBind
1
,

ST-HoBind
2
, and
ST-Trans
. The same
is true when we instantiate type parameters. For example, a type
application
{t}\,{C}
, where
t
has type
\forall{X\leq A}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{B}

and
C\leq A
, is now desugared to

{{t}\,{C}}\,{(\bar{\eta}_{K}(C))}
, i.e. only the second type
argument, which corresponds to the constraint parameter
X_{\sf c}
,
needs to be
\eta
-expanded, while the argument
C
for the parameter

X_{\sf n}
can be left as is. Since
X_{\sf c}
does not occur freely in
the codomain
B
of the desugared universal type, the overall type of
the desugared application is just
B[C/X_{\sf n}]
.
A clear drawback of this encoding is the necessary duplication of
bindings and the corresponding introduction and elimination forms
(abstraction, application). In addition, the kind

X_{\sf n}\intv_{K}A
of the constraint
X_{\sf c}
has inconsistent
bounds in general, which can be problematic.

There is an obvious alternative definition for the family of kinds

{*}_{K}
, namely
{*}_{K}=(\bot_{K})\intv_{K}\mskip 1.0mu{}(\top_{K})
.
The original definition, given in
Fig.
2
of the paper
(page
2
), has the advantage of being independent
of the definition of the higher-order extrema
\top_{K}
and

\bot_{K}
.
This allowed us to prove properties such as
Wf-KMax

and
Lemma
B.22
admissible without appealing to any of the
properties of higher-order extrema, and thereby avoid some cyclic
dependencies in the proofs of the latter. The alternative definition,
on the other hand, seems more intuitive. To conclude the section, we
show that the two definitions are equal for well-formed kinds
K
.

Lemma B.34.

The kind
{*}_{K}
is equal to the higher-order interval bounded by

\bot_{K}
and
\top_{K}
. If
\;\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
, then

\Gamma\vdash{*}_{K}=(\bot_{K})\intv_{K}\mskip 1.0mu{}(\top_{K})
.

Proof of
Lemma
B.34
.

By induction on the structure of
K
. The base case is immediate.
In the inductive step, we use
SK-HoIntv
and the

\beta
-rule
TEq-
\beta
.

Let
K=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}
. We want to show that

\Gamma\;\vdash\;({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{{*}_{K_{2}}}\;=\;({Y}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{({\bot_{({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}}}\,{Y})\intv_{K_{2}}\mskip 1.0mu{}({\top_{({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}}}\,{Y})}

for some
Y
that does not occur freely in

\mathsf{fv}(\bot_{({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}})
or

\mathsf{fv}(\top_{({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}})
. By the IH, we have

\Gamma\vdash{*}_{K_{2}}=(\bot_{K_{2}})\intv_{K_{2}}\mskip 1.0mu{}(\top_{K_{2}})
but
we need to adjust the bounds of the right-hand interval. We use the
following equation for the lower bound, and an similar one for the
upper bound.

(by definition)

\displaystyle\Gamma\;\vdash\;{\bot_{({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}}}\,{Y}\;

\displaystyle\equiv\;{\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}}.\,\bot_{K_{2}}}\,{Y}

(by
K-TMin
and
TEq-
\beta
)

\displaystyle=\;\bot_{K_{2}}[Y/X]

(
\alpha
-renaming)

\displaystyle\equiv\;\bot_{K_{2}}\hskip 50.00008pt:\;{*}_{K_{2}}.

By
SK-Refl-KEq
,
SK-HoIntv

and
SK-AntiSym
we obtain

\Gamma\;\vdash\;(\bot_{K_{2}})\intv_{{*}_{K_{2}}}\mskip 1.0mu{}(\top_{K_{2}})\;=\;({\bot_{({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}}}\,{Y})\intv_{{*}_{K_{2}}}\mskip 1.0mu{}({\top_{({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}}}\,{Y})

which we re-index using
Lemma
B.32
. We conclude
by
KEq-Refl
and
KEq-DArr
.
∎

Appendix C
Simple Kinding of Normal Types

This section introduces a system of
simplified kinding

judgments which provide a syntactic characterization of normal types
and allow us to establish important properties about hereditary
substitutions and normal forms, notably a pair of commutativity lemmas
(
Lemmas
C.11
and
C.21
) that play an
important role in the development of
§
5
in the paper.

The contents of this section are based on Chapter 4 of the first
author’s PhD dissertation. We refer the interested reader to the
dissertation for the full details
(
Stucki 2017
, Ch. 4)
.

C.1.
Preliminaries

Before we can introduce the simplified system, we need to introduce a
few auxiliary definitions and lemmas that were omitted from

§
4
in the paper.

We start with the definition of
shape contexts
– the type of
context used in our simplified kinding judgments.
Shape contexts
\gamma
,
\delta
are best thought of as typing
contexts consisting exclusively of type variable bindings
X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muk\;

with shape annotations
k
(as opposed to full kind annotations
K
).
Their grammar is defined as follows.

(Shape context)

\displaystyle\gamma,\delta

\displaystyle::=\varnothing\hskip 4.30554pt\big|\hskip 4.30554pt\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muk

As for full contexts, we assume that the variables bound in a shape
context are all distinct.
We write
\mathsf{dom}(\gamma)
for the set of variables bound in
\gamma
and

(\gamma,\delta)
for the concatenation of two shape contexts with
disjoint domains.

C.1.1.
Weak Equality

Recall that
weak equality
is an equivalence
A\approx B
on
types and kinds that identifies operator abstractions up to the shape
of their domain annotations.

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\lvert J\rvert\equiv\lvert K\rvert\quad\quad A\approx B\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J}.\,A\approx\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,B\end{array}}

(WEq-Abs)

Formally, weak equality is defined as the smallest congruence (w.r.t. to
all the type and kind formers) that includes both syntactic equality
and the above rule
WEq-Abs
.
It is easy to verify that weak equality is a congruence w.r.t. ordinary substitution.
In the following sections, we will show that weak equality is also a
congruence w.r.t. hereditary substitution,
\eta
-expansion and
normalization, and that weakly equal kinds
K_{1}\approx K_{2}
have equal
shapes
\lvert K_{1}\rvert\equiv\lvert K_{2}\rvert
.

C.1.2.
Properties of Hereditary Substitution

Since it is defined pointwise on spines, hereditary substitution
commutes with spine concatenation.

Lemma C.1.

(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{2})[E/{X}^{\,k}]\;\equiv\;(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{1}[E/{X}^{\,k}],\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{2}[E/{X}^{\,k}])
.

Just as for ordinary substitution, shapes are stable under hereditary
substitution.

Lemma C.2 (stability of shapes under hereditary
substitution).

\lvert J[A/{X}^{\,k}]\rvert\equiv\lvert J\rvert
.

Proof.

By straightforward induction on the structure of
J
.
∎

As a consequence, weak equality is a congruence w.r.t. hereditary
substitution and reducing application.

Lemma C.3.

Weak equality is a congruence w.r.t. hereditary substitution and
reducing application. Let
E_{1}\approx E_{2}
,

(1)

if
K_{1}\approx K_{2}
, then

K_{1}[E_{1}/{X}^{\,k}]\approx K_{2}[E_{2}/{X}^{\,k}]
;

(2)

if
D_{1}\approx D_{2}
, then

D_{1}[E_{1}/{X}^{\,k}]\approx D_{2}[E_{2}/{X}^{\,k}]
;

(3)

if
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\approx\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{2}
, then

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{1}[E_{1}/{X}^{\,k}]\approx\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{2}[E_{2}/{X}^{\,k}]
;

(4)

if
D_{1}\approx D_{2}
, then

{{E_{1}}\,\cdot^{k}}\,{D_{1}}\approx{{E_{2}}\,\cdot^{k}}\,{D_{2}}
;

(5)

if
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\approx\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{2}
, then

{{E_{1}}\,\cdot^{k}}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{1}}\approx{{E_{2}}\,\cdot^{k}}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{2}}
.

Proof.

The structure of the proof mirrors that of the recursive definitions
of hereditary substitution and reducing application. All five parts
are proven simultaneously, by induction on the structure of
k
.
Parts
1
–
3
proceed by an
inner induction on the derivations of
K_{1}\approx K_{2}
,

D_{1}\approx D_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\approx\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{2}
, respectively.
Parts
4
and
5
proceed by a case
analysis on the final rules used to derive
E_{1}\approx E_{2}
and

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\approx\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{2}
, respectively. Part
2
proceeds by a case analysis on the final
rule used to derive
F_{1}\approx F_{2}
, where
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are the
heads, respectively, of
E_{1}=F_{1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{1}
and

E_{2}=F_{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{2}
. In the case for
WEq-Abs
, we use
stability of kind simplification under hereditary substitution
(
Lemma
C.2
).
In the variable case,
we use the IH twice: first for part
3
to derive

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{1}[E_{1}/{X}^{\,k}]\approx\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{2}[E_{2}/{X}^{\,k}]
,
then for part
5
, to derive

{{E_{1}}\,\cdot^{k}}\,{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{1}[E_{1}/{X}^{\,k}])}\approx{{E_{2}}\,\cdot^{k}}\,{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{2}[E_{2}/{X}^{\,k}])}
. In the second
instance,
k
does not decrease nor is

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{1}[E_{1}/{X}^{\,k}]\approx\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}_{2}[E_{2}/{X}^{\,k}]

a sub-derivation of the current premise. This use of the IH
is nevertheless justified because any subsequent use of the IH for
part
2
in the proof of part
5

must occur after the use of the IH for part
4
, at
which point
k
has necessarily decreased.
∎

C.1.3.
Properties of
\eta
-Expansion and Normalization

Unsurprisingly, shapes are stable under normalization.

Lemma C.4 (stability of shapes under normalization).

\lvert\mathsf{nf}_{\Gamma}(K)\rvert\equiv\lvert K\rvert
.

Proof.

By straightforward induction on the structure of
K
.
∎

Extending kind simplification pointwise to contexts, we define

\lvert\Gamma\rvert
as

\displaystyle\lvert\varnothing\rvert

\displaystyle=\varnothing

\displaystyle\lvert\Gamma,x\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muA\rvert

\displaystyle=\lvert\Gamma\rvert

\displaystyle\lvert\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK\rvert

\displaystyle=\lvert\Gamma\rvert,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\lvert K\rvert

It is easy to see that context lookup commutes with simplification,
i.e.
\lvert\Gamma\rvert(X)=\lvert\Gamma(X)\rvert
, and that simplified
contexts are stable under (hereditary) substitution and
normalization, i.e.

\displaystyle\lvert\Gamma[A/X]\rvert

\displaystyle=\lvert\Gamma\rvert

\displaystyle\lvert\Gamma[A/{X}^{\,k}]\rvert

\displaystyle=\lvert\Gamma\rvert

\displaystyle\lvert\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\rvert

\displaystyle=\lvert\Gamma\rvert

The following two lemmas show that
\eta
-expansion and normalization
preserve weak equality. Importantly, this is true even if the
corresponding kinds and contexts, respectively, are not themselves
weakly equal but have equal shapes – a much weaker requirement.

Lemma C.5.

Weak equality is preserved by
\eta
-expansion along kinds of equal
shape. If
\lvert J\rvert\equiv\lvert K\rvert
and
D\approx E
, then

\eta_{J}(D)\approx\eta_{K}(E)
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
J
and case analysis on the final
rule used to derive
D\approx E
.
∎

Lemma C.6.

Kinds and types normalize weakly equally in contexts that simplify
equally. Let
\Gamma
and
\Delta
be contexts such that

\lvert\Gamma\rvert\equiv\lvert\Delta\rvert
. Then

(1)

\mathsf{nf}_{\Gamma}(K)\approx\mathsf{nf}_{\Delta}(K)
for any kind
K
, and

(2)

\mathsf{nf}_{\Gamma}(A)\approx\mathsf{nf}_{\Delta}(A)
for any type
A
.

Proof.

Simultaneously, by induction on the structure of
K
and
A
,
respectively. In the type variable case
A=X
we use

Lemma
C.5
; in the operator application case

A={A_{1}}\,{A_{2}}
we use
Lemma
C.3
.
2
;
in the cases for dependent operator kinds, universal types and
operator abstraction, we use
Lemma
C.18
.
∎

C.2.
The Simplified System

The function
\mathsf{nf}
assigns to each raw type
A
in a given context

\Gamma
a unique type
\mathsf{nf}_{\Gamma}(A)
. But as we have seen, the
type
\mathsf{nf}_{\Gamma}(A)
may not be
\beta\eta
-normal if
A
is
ill-kinded. In this section, we prove the converse: whenever
A
is
well-kinded in
\Gamma
, the type
\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(A)
is a

\eta
-long
\beta
-normal form. To do so, we first introduce a set
of
simplified kinding
judgments. Roughly, a simplified kinding
judgment
\gamma\vdash E:k
establishes that the type
E
is a
normal form of
shape

k
in the
shape context

\gamma
.
Given
\gamma\vdash E:k
, we say that
E
is a
well-shaped

or
simply (well-)kinded normal form
, or just that
E
is

simply kinded
. As we are about to show, every well-kinded type

\Gamma\vdash A:K
has a well-shaped normal form

\lvert\Gamma\rvert\vdash E:\lvert K\rvert
, namely

E=\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(A)
(see
Lemma
C.14
below).

It is important to note that the converse is not true: not every
simply kinded type is well-kinded. Because kind simplification
forgets dependencies, there are necessarily some ill-kinded types that
are considered simply well-kinded according to the judgments we are
about to introduce. However, every simply kinded type is guaranteed
to be an
\eta
-long
\beta
-normal form and, as we will see in this
section, simple kinding is preserved by operations such as hereditary
substitution and
\eta
-expansion. Hence simple kinding allows us to
prove important properties about these operations on

\beta\eta
-normal forms without subjecting ourselves to the
complexity of fully dependent kinds.

To enhance readability, we use the following naming conventions for
normal forms: the metavariables
U
,
V
,
W
denote normal types,
while
M
and
N
denote neutral types.
7
7

7

This is just
notation. We do not consider normal forms a separate syntactic
category, e.g. the letters
U
,
V
,
W
are metavariables
denoting types (typically in elimination form) rather than
non-terminals in some grammar of normal forms.
No special notation
is used for normal kinds.

Judgments.

Fig.
9
defines the following
judgments by mutual induction.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\gap}l}\gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kds}}\hfil\quad\quad&\text{the kind $K$ is simply well-formed and normal in $\gamma$}\\
\gamma\vdash V:k\hfil\quad\quad&\text{the type $V$ is a normal form of shape $k$ in $\gamma$}\\
\gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}N:k\hfil\quad\quad&\text{the type $N$ is a neutral form of shape $k$ in $\gamma$}\\
\gamma\vdash j:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}:k\hfil\quad\quad&\text{applying an operator of shape $j$ to the normal spine
$\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}$}\\
\hfil\quad\quad&\text{yields a type of shape $k$ in $\gamma$.}\end{array}

Simplified well-formedness of kinds

\gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kds}}

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash U:{*}\quad\quad\gamma\vdash V:{*}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash{U\intv V\;\mathsf{kds}}\end{array}}

(
SWf-Intv
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kds}}\quad\quad\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\lvert J\rvert\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kds}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash{({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{K}\;\mathsf{kds}}\end{array}}

(
SWf-DArr
)

Kinding of neutral types

\gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}N:k

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma(X)=j\quad\quad\gamma\vdash j:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}:k\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}{X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}:k\end{array}}

(
SK-VarApp
)

Simple spine kinding

\gamma\vdash j:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}:k

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash k:\epsilon:k\end{array}}

(
SK-Empty
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash U:j\quad\quad\gamma\vdash k:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}:l\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash j\rightarrow k:U,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}:l\end{array}}

(
SK-Cons
)

Simple kinding of normal types

\gamma\vdash V:k

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash\top:{*}\end{array}}

(
SK-Top
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash U:{*}\quad\quad\gamma\vdash V:{*}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash U\rightarrow V:{*}\end{array}}

(
SK-Arr
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kds}}\quad\quad\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\lvert J\rvert\vdash V:k\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J}.\,V:\lvert J\rvert\rightarrow k\end{array}}

(
SK-Abs
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash\bot:{*}\end{array}}

(
SK-Bot
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kds}}\quad\quad\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\lvert K\rvert\vdash V:{*}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{V}:{*}\end{array}}

(
SK-All
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}N:{*}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash N:{*}\end{array}}

(
SK-Ne
)

Figure 9
.
Simplified kinding
Simplified kinding

The judgments for simple kind formation and kinding follow the
syntactic structure of normal kinds and types. A type
V
is a

\beta\eta
-normal form
\gamma\vdash V:k
of shape
k
if it
is either a proper type introduced by one of the basic type formers
applied to normal arguments (rules
SK-Top
,
SK-Bot
,

SK-Arr
, and
SK-All
), an operator abstraction with
a normal body (rule
SK-Abs
), or a simply kinded
neutral type (rule
SK-Ne
). Simply kinded neutral forms

\gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}N:k
are eliminations headed by an abstract type
operator, i.e. a type variable
X
, which is applied to a spine of
normal types
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}
(rule
SK-VarApp
). Finally, a simply
well-formed normal kind
\gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kds}}
is either a type interval
bounded by normal types (rule
SWf-Intv
) or a dependent arrow
with normal domain and codomain (rule
SWf-DArr
). Note that
type operator abstractions are the only normal forms of arrow shape.
This ensures that normal types are always
\eta
-long.

The simple spine kinding judgment
\gamma\vdash j:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}:k
is
different from the other judgment forms in that it is a quaternary
rather than a ternary relation. The shapes
j
and
k
should be read
as inputs and outputs, respectively, of such judgments: when a type of
shape
j
is applied to the spine
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}
(the subject of the judgment), the resulting type
is of shape
k
– as exemplified by the rule
SK-VarApp
.

There is no formation judgment for shape contexts
\gamma
since such
contexts only contain bindings assigning shapes to type variables, and there is no such
thing as an ill-formed simple kind.

Because kinding is simplified, there is no notion of subkinding or
kind equality, and hence no need for a subsumption rule. As a
consequence, the simple kind formation and kinding rules are
syntax-directed.
Another important property of simplified kinding is that none of the
rules involve substitutions in kinds.
This substantially simplifies the proofs of key lemmas about
hereditary substitutions, in particular that of
Lemma
C.10

which states that hereditary substitutions preserve simple kinding
(and thus normal forms).
It is also important in establishing admissibility of the following
simple rules about spines and neutral types.

Lemma C.7.

The following simple kinding rules for spine concatenation and
application of neutrals are admissible.

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash j:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}:k\quad\quad\gamma\vdash k:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}:l\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash j:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}:l\end{array}}

(SK-Concat)

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash j:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}:k\rightarrow l\quad\quad\gamma\vdash V:k\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash j:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}},V:l\end{array}}
(SK-Snoc)

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}N:j\rightarrow k\quad\quad\gamma\vdash V:j\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}{N}\,{V}:k\end{array}}
(SK-NeApp)

Proof.

The proofs are done separately for each of the three rules in the
order the rules are listed. The proof for
SK-Concat
is by
induction on the derivation of the first premise. The
rule
SK-Cons
is derivable from
SK-Snoc
as a
special case where
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}=V,\epsilon
,
using
SK-Empty
and
SK-Cons
. The proof
of
SK-NeApp
starts with a case analysis on the final rule
used to derive
\Gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}N:j\rightarrow k
. The only rule for
deriving such judgments is
SK-VarApp
, hence
N
must be of
the form
N={X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}}
with
\gamma(X)=l
and

\gamma\vdash l:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}:j\rightarrow k
. We conclude
by
SK-Snoc
and
SK-VarApp
.
∎

C.2.1.
Simply-Kinded Hereditary Substitution

Before we can prove that hereditary substitutions preserve simple
kinding, we first need to establish the usual weakening properties for
simple kind formation and kinding.

Lemma C.8 (weakening).

A simple judgment remains true if its context is extended by an
additional binding. Let
\gamma
,
\delta
be shape contexts,
k
a
shape and
X\notin\mathsf{dom}(\gamma,\delta)
. If

\;\gamma,\delta\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
for any of the simple judgments defined
above, then
\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muk,\delta\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
.

Proof.

Simultaneously for all four judgments, by induction on the
derivation of
\;\gamma,\delta\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
.
∎

Corollary C.9 (Iterated weakening).

Given a pair
\gamma
,
\delta
of disjoint shape contexts, if

\;\gamma\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
, then
\gamma,\delta\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
.

Lemma C.10 (hereditary substitution).

Hereditary substitutions and reducing applications preserve the
shapes of types and simple well-formedness of kinds. Let
\gamma
,

\delta
be shape contexts and
X
such that

X\notin\mathsf{dom}(\gamma,\delta)
. Assume further that

\gamma\vdash V:k
for some
V
and
k
. Then

(1)

if

\;\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muk,\delta\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kds}}
, then

\gamma,\delta\vdash{J[V/{X}^{\,k}]\;\mathsf{kds}}
;

(2)

if

\;\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muk,\delta\vdash U:j
, then

\gamma,\delta\vdash U[V/{X}^{\,k}]:j
;

(3)

if

\;\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muk,\delta\vdash_{\sf ne}N:j
, then

\gamma,\delta\vdash N[V/{X}^{\,k}]:j
as a normal form;

(4)

if

\;\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muk,\delta\vdash j:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}:l
, then

\gamma,\delta\vdash j:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}[V/{X}^{\,k}]:l
;

(5)

if
k=k_{1}\rightarrow k_{2}
and

\;\gamma\vdash U:k_{1}
, then

\gamma\vdash{{V}\,\cdot^{k_{1}\rightarrow k_{2}}}\,{U}:k_{2}
;

(6)

if

\;\gamma\vdash k:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}:j
, then

\gamma\vdash{{V}\,\cdot^{k}}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}}:j
.

Note that hereditary substitutions preserve the shapes of neutral
types but not neutrality itself.

Proof.

All six parts are proven simultaneously by induction on the
structure of
k
.
Parts
1
–
4
proceed by an
inner induction on the simple formation or kinding derivations for

J
,
U
,
N
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}
, respectively.
Parts
5
and
6
proceed by a case
analysis on the final rules used to derive

\gamma\vdash V:k_{1}\rightarrow k_{2}
and

\gamma\vdash k:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}:j
, respectively; for
part
5
, the only applicable rule
is
SK-Abs
. For part
3
, in the case
for
SK-VarApp
when
N={X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}}
, we use iterated
weakening (
Corollary
C.9
) and the IH
(for
4
), respectively, to obtain

\gamma,\delta\vdash V:k
and

\gamma,\delta\vdash k:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}[V/{X}^{\,k}]:j
. To
conclude the case, we apply the IH again (for
6
).
In this second use of the IH,
k
does not decrease nor is

\gamma,\delta\vdash k:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}[V/{X}^{\,k}]:j
a strict
sub-derivation of the current premise. However, in order to use the
IH for part
3
again from within the proof of
part
6
, we must go through
part
5
, at which point
k
necessarily decreases.
Again, the structure of the proof mirrors that of the mutually
recursive definitions of hereditary substitution and reducing
application.
∎

Thanks to
Lemma
C.10
, we can now prove the following
commutativity lemma about hereditary substitutions, which will play an
important role in the proof of
Lemma
C.21
below and in

§
5
of the paper.

Lemma C.11 (commutativity of hereditary
substitutions).

Hereditary substitutions of simply kinded types commute; hereditary
substitutions of simply kinded types commute with simply kinded
reducing applications. Let
\gamma_{1}\vdash U:j
and

\gamma_{1},X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\gamma_{2}\vdash V:k
. Then

(1)

if

\;\gamma_{1},X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\gamma_{2},Y\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muk,\gamma_{3}\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kds}}
, then

J[V/{Y}^{\,k}][U/{X}^{\,j}]\;\equiv\;J[U/{X}^{\,j}][V[U/{X}^{\,j}]/{Y}^{\,k}];

(2)

if

\;\gamma_{1},X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\gamma_{2},Y\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muk,\gamma_{3}\vdash W:l
, then

W[V/{Y}^{\,k}][U/{X}^{\,j}]\;\equiv\;W[U/{X}^{\,j}][V[U/{X}^{\,j}]/{Y}^{\,k}];

(3)

if

\;\gamma_{1},X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\gamma_{2},Y\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muk,\gamma_{3}\vdash_{\sf ne}N:l
, then

N[V/{Y}^{\,k}][U/{X}^{\,j}]\;\equiv\;N[U/{X}^{\,j}][V[U/{X}^{\,j}]/{Y}^{\,k}];

(4)

if

\;\gamma_{1},X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\gamma_{2},Y\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muk,\gamma_{3}\vdash l_{1}:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}:l_{2}
,
then

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}[V/{Y}^{\,k}][U/{X}^{\,j}]\;\equiv\;\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}[U/{X}^{\,j}][V[U/{X}^{\,j}]/{Y}^{\,k}];

(5)

if
k=k_{1}\rightarrow k_{2}
and

\;\gamma_{1},X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\gamma_{2}\vdash W:k_{1}
, then

({{V}\,\cdot^{k_{1}\rightarrow k_{2}}}\,{W})[U/{X}^{\,j}]\;\equiv\;{{(V[U/{X}^{\,j}])}\,\cdot^{k_{1}\rightarrow k_{2}}}\,{(W[U/{X}^{\,j}])};

(6)

if

\;\gamma_{1},X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\gamma_{2}\vdash k:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}:l
, then

({{V}\,\cdot^{k}}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}})[U/{X}^{\,j}]\;\equiv\;{{(V[U/{X}^{\,j}])}\,\cdot^{k}}\,{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}[U/{X}^{\,j}])}
.

Proof.

All six parts are proven simultaneously by simultaneous induction on
the structures of
j
and
k
. Simultaneous structural induction on

j
and
k
means roughly that it is sufficient for either one of

j
or
k
to decrease in an induction step. More formally, denote
by
\sqsubseteq
the sub-expression order on shapes, then the
simultaneous induction order
<
on unordered pairs
\{j,k\}

of shapes is defined as
\{j_{1},j_{2}\}<\{k_{1},k_{2}\}
if

j_{1}\sqsubset k_{1}
and
j_{2}\sqsubseteq k_{2}
.
Importantly,
<
is defined over
unordered
pairs which allows
us to exchange
j
and
k
in an induction step.
Parts
1
–
4

proceed by an inner induction on the simple formation or kinding
derivations for
J
,
W
,
N
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}
, respectively.

As usual, the interesting cases are those for
part
3
, when
N={Y}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}}
and

N={X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}}
.

•

Case
SK-VarApp
,
N={Y}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}}
.
We
have

\gamma_{1},X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\gamma_{2},Y\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muk,\gamma_{3}\vdash k:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}:l
.
By
Lemma
C.10
.
4
, we obtain

\gamma_{1},X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\vdash k:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}[V/{Y}^{\,k}]:l
, and hence we have

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt({Y}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}})

\displaystyle[V/{Y}^{\,k}][U/{X}^{\,j}]

(by definition)

\displaystyle\equiv\;({{V}\,\cdot^{k}}\,{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}[V/{Y}^{\,k}])})[U/{X}^{\,j}]

(by the IH for
6
)

\displaystyle\equiv\;{{(V[U/{X}^{\,j}])}\,\cdot^{k}}\,{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}[V/{Y}^{\,k}][U/{X}^{\,j}])}

(by the IH for
4
)

\displaystyle\equiv\;{{(V[U/{X}^{\,j}])}\,\cdot^{k}}\,{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}[U/{X}^{\,j}][V[U/{X}^{\,j}]/{Y}^{\,k}])}

(by definition)

\displaystyle\equiv\;({Y}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}})[U/{X}^{\,j}][V[U/{X}^{\,j}]/{Y}^{\,k}].

•

Case
SK-VarApp
,
N={X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}}
.
We
have

\gamma_{1},X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\gamma_{2},Y\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muk,\gamma_{3}\vdash j:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}:l
.
By
Lemma
C.10
.
4
, we obtain

\gamma_{1},X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\vdash j:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}[V/{Y}^{\,k}]:l
, and hence we have

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt({X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}})

\displaystyle[V/{Y}^{\,k}][U/{X}^{\,j}]

(by definition)

\displaystyle\equiv\;({X}\,{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}[V/{Y}^{\,k}])})[U/{X}^{\,j}]

(by definition)

\displaystyle\equiv\;{{U}\,\cdot^{j}}\,{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}[V/{Y}^{\,k}][U/{X}^{\,j}])}

(by the IH for
4
)

\displaystyle\equiv\;{{U}\,\cdot^{j}}\,{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}[U/{X}^{\,j}][V[U/{X}^{\,j}]/{Y}^{\,k}])}

(as
Y\notin\mathsf{fv}(U)
)

\displaystyle\equiv\;{{(U[V[U/{X}^{\,j}]/{Y}^{\,k}])}\,\cdot^{j}}\,{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}[U/{X}^{\,j}][V[U/{X}^{\,j}]/{Y}^{\,k}])}

(by the IH for
6
)

\displaystyle\equiv\;({{U}\,\cdot^{j}}\,{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}[U/{X}^{\,j}])})[V[U/{X}^{\,j}]/{Y}^{\,k}]

(by definition)

\displaystyle\equiv\;({X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}})[U/{X}^{\,j}][V[U/{X}^{\,j}]/{Y}^{\,k}].

Note that, in the second case, we switched the roles of the shape

j
and
k
when invoking the IH for part
6
.
∎

C.2.2.
Simplification and Normalization of Kinding

Thanks to
Lemma
4.3
we know that the definition of the
normalization function
\mathsf{nf}
is sound, i.e. that well-formed kinds

\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
and well-kinded types
\Gamma\vdash A:K
are
convertible with
\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(K)
and

\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(A)
, respectively. But we have yet to establish
that
\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(K)
and
\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(A)
are
actually normal forms. In this section, we prove a more general
result, namely that, whenever
\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
and

\Gamma\vdash A:K
,
it follows that
\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(K)
is a simply well-formed
normal kind and and
\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(A)
is a simply well-kinded
normal type.

As a first step, we show that the shapes of variables and, more
generally, of neutral types are preserved by
\eta
-expansion.

Lemma C.12.

\eta
-expansion preserves the shapes of neutral types.
Assume
\gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kds}}
and
\gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}N:\lvert K\rvert
.
Then
\gamma\vdash\eta_{K}(N):\lvert K\rvert
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
K
. The case fore

K=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}
proceeds by case analysis on the final
rules used to derive
\gamma\vdash{({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}\;\mathsf{kds}}
and

\gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}N:\lvert K_{1}\rvert\rightarrow\lvert K_{2}\rvert
and uses the
weakening lemma (
Lemma
C.8
) as well as
SK-NeApp
.
∎

Next, we require a syntactic notion of normal contexts. We define the

simple context formation
judgment
{\Gamma\;\mathsf{ctxs}}
as the
pointwise lifting of simple kind formation and kinding to bindings:

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\varnothing\;\mathsf{ctxs}}\end{array}}

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\Gamma\;\mathsf{ctxs}}\quad\quad\lvert\Gamma\rvert\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kds}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK\;\mathsf{ctxs}}\end{array}}

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\Gamma\;\mathsf{ctxs}}\quad\quad\lvert\Gamma\rvert\vdash V:{*}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\Gamma,x\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muV\;\mathsf{ctxs}}\end{array}}

Since simple kind formation and kinding is defined on normal kinds and
types, a simply well-formed context
\Gamma
is also normal.
Conversely, if we lookup the declared kind or type of a variable in a
simply well-formed context, the result is guaranteed to be a normal
form.

Lemma C.13.

The declared kinds and types of variables in a simply well-formed
context
\Gamma
are simply well-formed and well-kinded,
respectively, in
\lvert\Gamma\rvert
, i.e

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\;\mathsf{ctxs}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\lvert\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\rvert\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kds}}\end{array}}

(SC-TpLookup)

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\Gamma,x\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muV,\Delta\;\mathsf{ctxs}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\lvert\Gamma,x\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muV,\Delta\rvert\vdash V:{*}\end{array}}
(SC-TmLookup)

Proof.

Both parts are proven separately by structural induction on
\Delta

and case analysis on the final rule used to derive the premise. In
the inductive case, we use the weakening lemma for simple kind
formation.
∎

With
Lemmas
C.12
and
C.13
at hand, it is
easy to show that
\mathsf{nf}
does indeed produce normal forms.

Lemma C.14 (normalization and simplification).

Well-formed kinds and well-kinded types have simply well-formed and
simply kinded normal forms, respectively.

(1)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
, then

\lvert\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\rvert\vdash{\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(K)\;\mathsf{kds}}
.

(2)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash A:K
, then

\lvert\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\rvert\vdash\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(A):\lvert K\rvert
.

(3)

If
\;{\Gamma\;\mathsf{ctx}}
, then

{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\;\mathsf{ctxs}}
.

The proof uses the following helper lemma about the shapes of
subkinds, which is proven by straightforward induction on subkinding
derivations.

Lemma C.15.

Subkinds have equal shapes. If
\;\Gamma\vdash J\leq K
, then

\lvert J\rvert\equiv\lvert K\rvert
.

Proof of
Lemma
C.14
.

Simultaneously by induction on declarative kind formation, kinding,
and context formation derivations. The only interesting cases
are
K-Sub
(where we use
Lemma
C.15
),

K-Var
and
K-App
. In the case for

K-Var
, where
A=X
, we use the IH for
part
3
and
SC-TpLookup
to obtain

\lvert\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\rvert\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kds}}
for
K=\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)(X)
, and
we conclude by
Lemma
C.12
. In the case for
K-App
,
where
A={A_{1}}\,{A_{2}}
, we start by applying the IH to obtain

\lvert\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\rvert\vdash\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(A_{1}):\lvert K_{1}\rvert\rightarrow\lvert K_{2}\rvert
, and

\lvert\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\rvert\vdash\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(A_{2}):\lvert K_{1}\rvert
.
The first of these judgments must be derived using
SK-Abs

because that is the only simple kinding rule assigning an arrow kind
to a type. Hence
\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(A_{1})=\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J}.\,V
for
some
J
and
V
such that
\lvert J\rvert\equiv\lvert K_{1}\rvert
and

\lvert\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\rvert,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\lvert K_{1}\rvert\vdash V:\lvert K_{2}\rvert
.
We conclude by the hereditary substitution lemma for normal types
(
Lemma
C.10
.
2
) and

Lemma
C.2
.
∎

C.2.3.
Commutativity of Normalization and Substitution

Our final task in this section is to establish another commutativity
property that will play a crucial role in proving equality of
declarative and canonical subtyping: the fact that normalization
commutes with substitution.

In the past few sections, we have seen that well-formed kinds and
well-kinded types have normal forms (
Lemma
C.14
) and that these
normal forms are convertible to the kinds and types they were computed
from (
Lemma
4.3
). By validity, context conversion and kind
conversion, this means that every declarative subtyping judgment

\Gamma\vdash A\leq B:K
has an associated judgment

\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\vdash\mathsf{nf}(A)\leq\mathsf{nf}(B):\mathsf{nf}(K)
relating
the normal forms of the original expressions.

In
§
5
of the paper, we describe a system of canonical
rules for deriving such judgments which are defined directly on normal
forms – similar to the simple kinding and kind formation judgments
introduced in this section.
The proof of equivalence of the two systems requires one canonical
rule
– possibly a derivable or admissible one –
for every declarative rule.
But some of the declarative rules, such as the subtyping
rules
ST-
\beta_{1,2}
for
\beta
-conversions,
or the kinding rule
K-App
for applications, involve
substitutions, which do not preserve normal forms.
To see why this is
a problem, consider the declarative rule
K-App
:

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A:({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{K}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash B:J\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{A}\,{B}:K[B/X]\end{array}}

By soundness of normalization (
Lemma
4.3
), type equation validity
(
Lemma
3.1
), and context conversion (
Corollary
B.13
),
we know that the following is also admissible:

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\vdash U:({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J^{\prime}})\rightarrow{K^{\prime}}\quad\quad\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\vdash V:J^{\prime}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\vdash\mathsf{nf}({A}\,{B}):\mathsf{nf}(K[B/X])\end{array}}

where
U=\mathsf{nf}(A)
,
V=\mathsf{nf}(B)
,

J^{\prime}=\mathsf{nf}(J)
and
K^{\prime}=\mathsf{nf}(K)
. By
Lemma
C.14
, we know that

U
and
V
are simply well-kinded normal types, and that
J^{\prime}
and

K^{\prime}
are simply well-formed normal kinds. For our canonical
application rule, we would like to express the type

\mathsf{nf}({A}\,{B})
and the kind
\mathsf{nf}(K[B/X])
in the
conclusion directly using
U
,
V
,
J^{\prime}
and
K^{\prime}
. This is relatively
straightforward for the application
\mathsf{nf}({A}\,{B})
because we
know that
U
must be an operator abstraction
U=\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{L}.\,W
;
after all,
U
has shape
\lvert J\rvert\rightarrow\lvert K\rvert
and normal
forms are
\eta
-long. We also know that

\lvert L\rvert\equiv\lvert J\rvert\equiv\lvert J^{\prime}\rvert
(see the proof of

Lemma
C.14
for details). Hence

\mathsf{nf}({A}\,{B})\equiv W[V/{X}^{\,\lvert J^{\prime}\rvert}]
by definition
of
\mathsf{nf}
, and we are done.

Things are more complicated for the normal kind

\mathsf{nf}(K[B/X])
. The definition of the normalization
function
\mathsf{nf}
does not tell us anything immediately useful about
substitutions. Indeed, we know that substitutions do not preserve
normal forms, e.g.
({Y}\,{V})[\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J^{\prime}}.\,W/Y]
is not a
normal form, even if
{Y}\,{V}
and
\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J^{\prime}}.\,W
are. However,

Corollary
4.2
.
1
tells us that substitutions
in kinds are judgmentally equal to hereditary substitutions, i.e.
\Gamma\vdash K[B/X]=K[B/{X}^{\,\lvert J^{\prime}\rvert}]
, and

Lemma
C.10
.
1
tells us that hereditary
substitutions preserve normal forms, all of which suggests that

\mathsf{nf}(K[B/X])
should be equal to

K^{\prime}[V/{X}^{\,\lvert J^{\prime}\rvert}]
. This is indeed the case; one can
show that

\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{nf}(K[B/X])=K^{\prime}[V/{X}^{\,\lvert J^{\prime}\rvert}]
.
But there is a caveat: the two normal forms are not

syntactically
equal, i.e.
\mathsf{nf}(K[B/X])\,\not\equiv\,K^{\prime}[V/{X}^{\,\lvert J^{\prime}\rvert}]
.
Similarly,

\mathsf{nf}(A[B/X])\,\not\equiv\,\mathsf{nf}(A)[\mathsf{nf}(B)/{X}^{\,k}]

for types
A
and
B
in general.

This fact is best illustrated through the case of type variables,
i.e. when
A=X
and we have

\mathsf{nf}_{\Gamma}(X[B/X])\equiv\mathsf{nf}_{\Gamma}(B)
and

(\mathsf{nf}_{\Gamma}(X))[\mathsf{nf}(B)/{X}^{\,k}]\equiv(\eta_{\Gamma(X)}(X))[\mathsf{nf}(B)/{X}^{\,k}]
.
We would like to show that
(\eta_{K}(X))[V/{X}^{\,k}]
is
syntactically equal to
V
at least when all the involved types and
kinds are well-kinded and well-formed, i.e. when

\Gamma\vdash X:K
,
\;\Gamma\vdash V:K
and
k=\lvert K\rvert
.
But this is not the case. The culprit is a mismatch of kind
annotations in operator abstractions, as illustrated by the following
counterexample.

Let
J_{1}=\top\intv\top
and
J_{2}={*}
so that

\Gamma\vdash J_{1}\leq J_{2}
for any context
\Gamma
. Let
U=\top
,

V=\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{2}}.\,U
and
K=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}})\rightarrow{{*}}
so that

\Gamma\vdash V:({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{2}})\rightarrow{{*}}
,

\Gamma\vdash({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{2}})\rightarrow{{*}}\leq K
and hence

\Gamma\vdash V:K
. Then

\displaystyle\eta_{K}(Y)

\displaystyle\;\equiv\;\lambda{Z}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}}.\,{Y}\,{Z}

\displaystyle\eta_{K}(Y)

\displaystyle[V/{Y}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]

\displaystyle\;\equiv\;(\lambda{Z}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}}.\,{Y}\,{Z})[V/{Y}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]

(because
Y\notin\mathsf{fv}(J_{1})
)

\displaystyle\;\equiv\;\lambda{Z}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}}.\,({Y}\,{Z})[V/{Y}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]

\displaystyle\;\equiv\;\lambda{Z}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}}.\,{{V}\,\cdot^{\lvert K\rvert}}\,{Z}

\displaystyle\;\equiv\;\lambda{Z}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}}.\,{{\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{2}}.\,U}\,\cdot^{\lvert J_{1}\rvert\rightarrow{*}}}\,{Z}

\displaystyle\;\equiv\;\lambda{Z}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}}.\,U[Z/{X}^{\,\lvert J_{1}\rvert}]

(as
X,Z\notin\mathsf{fv}(U)
)

\displaystyle\;\equiv\;\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}}.\,U

(because
J_{1}\not\equiv J_{2}
)

\displaystyle\;\not\equiv\;\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{2}}.\,U\;\equiv\;V.

So we are forced to conclude that

\eta_{K}(Y)[V/{Y}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\not\equiv V
in general. The
problem, as illustrated by this example, is that the domain annotation

J_{2}
of the type operator abstraction
V=\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{2}}.\,U
is not
necessarily preserved by the hereditary substitution. It is replaced
by the domain
J_{1}
of the declared kind
K=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}})\rightarrow{U}
of

Y
, which need not be
syntactically
equal to
J_{1}
.

However, we do have
\Gamma\vdash J_{1}\leq J_{2}
and thus

\lvert J_{1}\rvert\equiv\lvert J_{2}\rvert
. The solution, therefore, is to be more lenient
when comparing domain annotations in operator abstractions: the

weak
equation
\eta_{K}(Y)[V/{Y}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\approx V

does hold. In fact, it holds for any simply well-formed kind
K
and
simply well-kinded type
U
, as the following lemma shows.

Lemma C.16.

(1)

Let

\;\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\delta\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kds}}
,
\;\gamma\vdash U:j

and

\;\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\delta\vdash_{\sf ne}{X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}:\lvert K\rvert
.
Then

(\eta_{K}({X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}))[U/{X}^{\,j}]\;\approx\;({X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}})[U/{X}^{\,j}].

Let
\gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kds}}
, then

(2)

if

\;\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\lvert K\rvert,\delta\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kds}}
, then

J[\eta_{K}(X)/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\;\approx\;J
;

(3)

if

\;\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\lvert K\rvert,\delta\vdash V:j
, then

V[\eta_{K}(X)/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\;\approx\;V
;

(4)

if

\;\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\lvert K\rvert,\delta\vdash j:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}:l
, then

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}[\eta_{K}(X)/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\;\approx\;\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}
;

(5)

if
\;K=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}
,

\;\gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}N:\lvert K\rvert
and

\;\gamma\vdash V:\lvert K_{1}\rvert
, then

\;{{\eta_{K}(N)}\,\cdot^{\lvert K\rvert}}\,{V}\;\approx\;\eta_{K_{2}[V/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}]}({N}\,{V})
;

(6)

if
\;\gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}N:\lvert K\rvert

and
\gamma\vdash\lvert K\rvert:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}:{*}
, then

{{\eta_{K}(N)}\,\cdot^{\lvert K\rvert}}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}\;\approx\;{N}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}
.

Corollary C.17.

If
\;\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\delta\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kds}}
and

\;\gamma\vdash U:j
, then

(\eta_{K}(X))[U/{X}^{\,j}]\;\approx\;U
.

It is in this lemma that we see the true usefulness of weak equality.
While syntactic equality is too strict for this particular
commutativity property, using judgmental type and kind equality would
have forced us to formulate its premises in terms of declarative
kinding. This would have resulted in a weaker lemma with a more
complicated proof.
In
§
D.2.2
we show that weak equations can be converted
into judgmental ones provided the related types or kinds are
well-kinded or well-formed, respectively. Hence, weak equality
affords us a relatively straightforward proof of this lemma (and the
next) with a minimal overhead in complexity.

In the proof of
Lemma
C.16
, we employ the following helper
lemmas.
They are proven separately by three easy inductions:
the first on the derivation of
K_{1}\approx K_{2}
, the second on the
structure of the kind
K
, and the third on the derivation of

\;\gamma\vdash j:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}:k
.

Lemma C.18.

Weakly equal kinds have equal shapes. If
K_{1}\approx K_{2}
, then

\lvert K_{1}\rvert\equiv\lvert K_{2}\rvert
.

Lemma C.19.

Let
X\neq Y
, then

(\eta_{K}({X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}}))[E/{Y}^{\,j}]\;\equiv\;\eta_{K[E/{Y}^{\,j}]}({X}\,{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}[E/{Y}^{\,j}])})

for any
K
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{D}}
,
j
and
E
.

Lemma C.20.

Let
\;\gamma\vdash U:j
,
\gamma\vdash j:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}:k
and

\gamma\vdash k:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}:l
, then

\;{{U}\,\cdot^{j}}\,{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}})}\;\equiv\;{{({{U}\,\cdot^{j}}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}})}\,\cdot^{k}}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}}.

Proof of
Lemma
C.16
.

All six parts are proven simultaneously by induction on the
structure of
K
.
Parts
2
–
4
proceed by
an inner induction on the simple formation and kinding derivations
for
J
,
V
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}
, respectively. We show a few key cases,
the remainder of the proof is routine.

•

Part
1
,
K=({Y}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}
.

By inspection of the formation and kinding rules, we must have

\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\delta\vdash{K_{1}\;\mathsf{kds}}
,

\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\delta,Y\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\lvert K_{1}\rvert\vdash{K_{2}\;\mathsf{kds}}
and

\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\delta\vdash j:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}:\lvert K\rvert
.
By
Lemma
C.10
we have

\gamma,\delta\vdash({X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}})[U/{X}^{\,j}]:\lvert K_{1}\rvert\rightarrow\lvert K_{2}\rvert\qquad\text{and}\qquad\gamma,\delta\vdash{K_{1}[U/{X}^{\,j}]\;\mathsf{kds}}.

The final kinding rule used to derive the first of these judgments
must be
SK-Abs
since that is the only rule assigning
arrow shapes to normal types. Therefore, the following must
hold for some
J
and
W
:

(3)

\displaystyle({X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}})[U/{X}^{\,j}]

\displaystyle=\lambda{Y}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J}.\,W

(4)

\displaystyle\lvert K_{1}\rvert

\displaystyle=\lvert J\rvert

(5)

\displaystyle\gamma,\delta,Y\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\lvert J\rvert

\displaystyle\vdash W:\lvert K_{2}\rvert.

By weakening (
Lemma
C.8
),
Lemma
C.12

and
SK-NeApp
we also have

\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muj,\delta,Y\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\lvert K_{1}\rvert\vdash_{\sf ne}{{X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}}\,{(\eta_{K_{1}}(Y))}:\lvert K_{2}\rvert

and hence

\displaystyle(\eta_{K_{2}}({{X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}}\,{(\eta_{K_{1}}(Y))}))[U/{X}^{\,j}]\hskip-100.00015pt

(by IH for
1
)

\displaystyle\approx\;({{X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}}\,{(\eta_{K_{1}}(Y))})[U/{X}^{\,j}]

(by definition)

\displaystyle\equiv\;{{U}\,\cdot^{j}}\,{((\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}},(\eta_{K_{1}}(Y)))[U/{X}^{\,j}])}

(by
Lemmas
C.1
and
C.20
)

\displaystyle\equiv\;{{({{U}\,\cdot^{j}}\,{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}[U/{X}^{\,j}])})}\,\cdot^{\lvert K\rvert}}\,{((\eta_{K_{1}}(Y))[U/{X}^{\,j}])}

(by
Lemma
C.19
)

\displaystyle\equiv\;{{({{U}\,\cdot^{j}}\,{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}[U/{X}^{\,j}])})}\,\cdot^{\lvert K\rvert}}\,{(\eta_{K_{1}[U/{X}^{\,j}]}(Y))}

(by definition)

\displaystyle\equiv\;{{(({X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}})[U/{X}^{\,j}])}\,\cdot^{\lvert K_{1}\rvert\rightarrow\lvert K_{2}\rvert}}\,{(\eta_{K_{1}[U/{X}^{\,j}]}(Y))}

(by (
3
))

\displaystyle\equiv\;{{(\lambda{Y}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J}.\,W)}\,\cdot^{\lvert K_{1}\rvert\rightarrow\lvert K_{2}\rvert}}\,{(\eta_{K_{1}[U/{X}^{\,j}]}(Y))}

(by definition)

\displaystyle\equiv\;W[(\eta_{K_{1}[U/{X}^{\,j}]}(Y))/{Y}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}]

(by
Lemma
C.18
, (
5
) and
IH for
3
)

\displaystyle\approx\;W

We conclude that

\displaystyle(\eta_{K}({X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}))[U/{X}^{\,j}]\hskip-60.00009pt

(by definition)

\displaystyle\equiv\;\lambda{Y}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}[U/{X}^{\,j}]}.\,(\eta_{K_{2}}({{X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}}\,{(\eta_{K_{1}}(Y))}))[U/{X}^{\,j}]

(by
Lemma
C.18
, (
4
),
the above and

)

\displaystyle\approx\;\lambda{Y}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J}.\,W

(by (
3
))

\displaystyle\equiv\;({X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}})[U/{X}^{\,j}].

•

Part
3
, case
SK-Ne
.

The rule
SK-Ne
has only one premise which must have been

, so
V=N={Y}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}

and we have

\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\lvert K\rvert,\delta\vdash j:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}:{*}
with

(\gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\lvert K\rvert,\delta)(Y)=j
. We distinguish two

Y=X
and

but consider only the first case
here; the second case is simpler. Since
Y=X
, we have

, and

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle[\eta_{K}(X)/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]

(by definition)

\displaystyle\equiv\;{{\eta_{K}(X)}\,\cdot^{\lvert K\rvert}}\,{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}[\eta_{K}(X)/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}])}

(by reflexivity of
\approx
, IH for
4

and
Lemma
C.3
.
5
)

\displaystyle\approx\;{{\eta_{K}(X)}\,\cdot^{\lvert K\rvert}}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}

(by

and the IH for

)

\displaystyle\approx\;{X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}.

•

Part
5
,
K=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}
.

By inspection of the formation and kinding rules, we must have

\gamma\vdash{K_{1}\;\mathsf{kds}}
and
N={Y}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}}
with

\gamma\vdash\gamma(Y):\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}:\lvert K\rvert
.

(by definition)

\displaystyle{{\eta_{K}(N)}\,\cdot^{\lvert K\rvert}}\,{V}

\displaystyle\equiv\;{{\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}}.\,\eta_{K_{2}}({{Y}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}}}\,{(\eta_{K_{1}}(X))})}\,\cdot^{\lvert K\rvert}}\,{V}

(by definition)

\displaystyle\equiv\;\eta_{K_{2}}({{Y}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}}}\,{(\eta_{K_{1}}(X))})[V/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}]

(by

)

\displaystyle\equiv\;\eta_{K_{2}[V/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}]}({Y}\,{((\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}},(\eta_{K_{1}}(X)))[V/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}])})

(as
X\notin\mathsf{fv}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}})
)

\displaystyle\equiv\;\eta_{K_{2}[V/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}]}({{Y}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}}}\,{((\eta_{K_{1}}(X))[V/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}])})

(by IH for
1
and

C.5
)

\displaystyle\approx\;\eta_{K_{2}[V/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}]}({{Y}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}}}\,{V}).

The use of the IH in the last step corresponds to

Corollary
C.17
.
∎

With

Lemma
C.16
in place, we are ready to prove that normalization
weakly commutes with substitution. In the following,

\Gamma\approx\Delta
denotes the pointwise lifting of weak equality
to contexts.

Lemma C.21.

Substitution weakly commutes with normalization of well-formed kinds
and well-kinded types. Let
\;\Gamma\vdash A:J
and

V=\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(A)
, then

(1)

if

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ,\Delta\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
, then

\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,\Delta[A/X])}(K[A/X])\;\approx\;(\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ,\Delta)}(K))[V/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}]
;

(2)

if

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ,\Delta\vdash B:K
, then

\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,\Delta[A/X])}(B[A/X])\;\approx\;(\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ,\Delta)}(B))[V/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}]
;

Proof.

Simultaneously by induction on declarative kind formation and
kinding derivations.
In the case for
K-Var
where
B=Y
, we use

Corollary
C.17
if
Y=X
.
Otherwise, let
\Sigma=\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ,\Delta
and

\Sigma^{\prime}=\Gamma,\Delta[A/X]
.
By stability of simplified contexts under normalization and
hereditary substitution, and by commutation of simplification and
context lookup, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\mathsf{nf}(\Sigma^{\prime})(Y)\rvert

\displaystyle\;\equiv\;\lvert\mathsf{nf}(\Sigma^{\prime})\rvert(Y)\;\equiv\;\lvert\Sigma^{\prime}\rvert(Y)

\displaystyle\;\equiv\;(\lvert\Gamma\rvert,\lvert\Delta[A/X]\rvert)(Y)\;\equiv\;(\lvert\Gamma\rvert,\lvert\Delta\rvert)(Y)\;\equiv\;(\lvert\Gamma\rvert,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\lvert J\rvert,\lvert\Delta\rvert)(Y)\;\equiv\;\lvert\Sigma\rvert(Y)

\displaystyle\;\equiv\;\lvert\mathsf{nf}(\Sigma)\rvert(Y)\;\equiv\;\lvert\mathsf{nf}(\Sigma)(Y)\rvert\;\equiv\;\lvert\mathsf{nf}_{\Sigma}(\Sigma(Y))\rvert\;\equiv\;\lvert(\mathsf{nf}_{\Sigma}(\Sigma(Y)))[V/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}]\rvert

Hence, by
Lemma
C.5
and
Lemma
C.19
we have

\eta_{\mathsf{nf}(\Sigma^{\prime})(Y)}(Y)\;\approx\;\eta_{(\mathsf{nf}_{\Sigma}(\Sigma(Y)))[V/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}]}(Y)\;\equiv\;(\eta_{\mathsf{nf}_{\Sigma}(\Sigma(Y))}(Y))[V/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}].

In the case for
K-App
, we use

Lemma
C.3
.
2
and

Lemma
C.11
.
2
.
∎

The very last lemma of this section will be used in our equivalence
proof in
§
D.3
to show that subtyping rules for

\eta
-conversion of normal operators are admissible in canonical
kinding.

Lemma C.22.

If
\;\Gamma\vdash A:({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{K}
with
X\notin\mathsf{fv}(A)
,
then

\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J}.\,{A}\,{X})\approx\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(A)
.

Proof.

The proof uses
Lemma
C.14
to obtain

\;\lvert\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\rvert\vdash\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(A):\lvert J\rvert\rightarrow\lvert K\rvert

and proceeds by case analysis on the final rule used to derive this
simple kinding judgment; the only applicable rule
is
SK-Abs
. The remainder of the proof uses equational
reasoning very similar to that used in the proof of

Lemma
C.16
.
1
.
∎

Appendix D
Properties of the Canonical System

This section states and proves some basic yet important metatheoretic
properties of the canonical system that have been omitted from

§
5
of the paper.
As a first step, we prove soundness of the canonical
system in
§
D.1
.
Next, we state and prove a
hereditary substitution lemma
in

§
D.2
, which establishes that canonical judgments
are preserved by hereditary substitutions.
This lemma is a key ingredient in proving completeness of the
canonical system.
We prove completeness in
§
D.3
, after showing that
canonical subtyping can be inverted at the top-level in

§
D.4
.

Fig.
10
shows the canonical judgments that have been
omitted from the paper: context and kind formation, subkinding, and
kind and type equality.

Context formation

{\Gamma\;\mathsf{ctx}}

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\varnothing\;\mathsf{ctx}}\end{array}}

(
CC-Empty
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\Gamma\;\mathsf{ctx}}\quad\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK\;\mathsf{ctx}}\end{array}}

(
CC-TmBind
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\Gamma\;\mathsf{ctx}}\quad\Gamma\vdash V\,\rightrightarrows\,V\intv V\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\Gamma,x\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muV\;\mathsf{ctx}}\end{array}}

(
CC-TpBind
)

Kind formation

\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\,\rightrightarrows\,U\intv U\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash V\,\rightrightarrows\,V\intv V\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{U\intv V\;\mathsf{kd}}\end{array}}

(
CWf-Intv
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kd}}\quad\quad\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{K}\;\mathsf{kd}}\end{array}}

)

Subkinding

\Gamma\vdash J\leq K

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\
\Gamma\vdash U_{2}\leq U_{1}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash V_{1}\leq V_{2}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U_{1}\intv V_{1}\leq U_{2}\intv V_{2}\end{array}}

(

)

The contents of this section are based on Chapter 5 of the first
author’s PhD dissertation. We refer the interested reader to the
