.

D.1.
Soundness and Basic Properties

Before we establish any other metatheoretic properties of the
canonical system, let us prove its soundness with respect to the
declarative presentation. To avoid confusion, we mark canonical
judgments with the subscript “
\mathsf{c}
” and declarative ones
with “
\mathsf{d}
” in the following lemma.

Lemma D.1 (soundness of the canonical rules).

(1)

If
\;{\Gamma\;\mathsf{ctx}_{\sf c}}
, then

\;{\Gamma\;\mathsf{ctx}_{\sf d}}
.

(2)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf c}{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
, then

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf d}{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
.

(3)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf c}J\leq K
, then

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf d}J\leq K
.

(4)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf c}J=K
, then

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf d}J=K
.

(5)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf var}X:K
, then

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf d}X:K
.

(6)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}N:K
, then

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf d}N:K
.

(7)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf c}V\,\rightrightarrows\,K
, then

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf d}V:K
.

(8)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf c}V\,\leftleftarrows\,K
, then

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf d}V:K
.

(9)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf c}U\leq V
, then

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf d}U\leq V:{*}
.

(10)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf c}U\leq V:K
, then

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf d}U\leq V:K
.

(11)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf c}U=V:K
, then

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf d}U\leq V:K
.

(12)

If

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf d}A:J
and

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf c}J\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}\,\rightrightarrows\,K
, then

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf d}{A}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}:K
.

(13)

If

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf d}A\leq B:J
and

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf c}J\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}\,\rightrightarrows\,K
, then

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf d}{A}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}}\leq{B}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}:K
.

The proof uses the following two helper lemmas.
The first is a derived rule, the second is proven by easy case
analysis of the rules for kind synthesis.

Lemma D.2.

If
\;\Gamma\vdash A:B\intv C
, then also

\Gamma\vdash A:{*}
.

Lemma D.3.

If
\;\Gamma\vdash U\,\rightrightarrows\,V\intv W
, then
V\equiv U
and

W\equiv U
.

Proof of
Lemma
D.1
.

By induction on derivations of the various canonical judgments; for
parts
1
–
11
, on the derivation of
the first premise, for parts
12

and
13
, on that of the second premise. Most cases
are straightforward. In cases involving synthesized kinding of
proper types, we use
K-Sing
and
Lemmas
D.2
,

B.15
and
D.3
to adjust kinds
where necessary. In cases involving hereditary substitutions, i.e. those for
CK-Cons
and
SpEq-Cons
, we use
soundness of hereditary substitution in kinds
(
Corollary
4.2
.
1
).
∎

As for the declarative system, the contexts of most canonical
judgments are well-formed. There are two exceptions: kinding and
equality of spines. The rules
CK-Empty

and
SpEq-Empty
for empty spines offer no guarantee that the
enclosing context is well-formed. This is not a problem in practice,
since well-kinded spines only appear in judgments about neutral types,
the heads of which must be kinded in a well-formed context.

Lemma D.4 (context validity).

Assume
\;\Gamma\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
for any canonical judgment except spine
kinding or equality. Then
\;{\Gamma\;\mathsf{ctx}}
.

Proof.

By simultaneous induction on the derivations of the various
judgments.
∎

We can prove a few more validity properties at this point.
First, since synthesized kinds are singletons, validity of synthesized
kinding judgments follows by
CWf-Intv
for proper types and
by an easy induction for type operators.
Second, validity of kind equality as well as the checked subtyping and
type equality judgments follows immediately from the validity
conditions included in the rules
CSK-AntiSym
,

CST-Intv
,
CST-Abs
and
CST-AntiSym
.

Lemma D.5 (canonical validity – part 1).

(synthesized kinding validity)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash V\,\rightrightarrows\,K
, then
\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
.

(checked subtyping validity)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash U\leq V\,\leftleftarrows\,K
, then
\Gamma\vdash U\,\leftleftarrows\,K
and

\Gamma\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,K
.

(kind equation validity)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash J=K
, then
\Gamma\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kd}}
and

\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
.

(type equation validity)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash U=V\,\leftleftarrows\,K
, then
\Gamma\vdash U\,\leftleftarrows\,K
,

\;\Gamma\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,K
and
\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
.

We prove the remaining validity properties of the canonical system
in
§
D.2.1
, once we have shown that hereditary substitutions
preserve well-formedness of kinds.

Before we can do so, we need to establish the usual weakening and
context narrowing lemmas for canonical typing.

Lemma D.6 (weakening).

Assume
\;\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
for any of the canonical
judgments.

(1)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash A\,\rightrightarrows\,A\intv A
, then

\;\Gamma,x\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muA,\Delta\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
.

(2)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
, then

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
.

Corollary D.7 (iterated weakening).

If
\;{\Gamma,\Delta\;\mathsf{ctx}}
and
\Gamma\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
, then

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
.

Lemma D.8 (context narrowing – weak version).

(1)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash A\,\rightrightarrows\,A\intv A
,
\;\Gamma\vdash A\leq B:{*}

and
\;\Gamma,x\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muB,\Delta\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
, then

\;\Gamma,x\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muA,\Delta\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
.

(2)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kd}}
,
\;\Gamma\vdash J\leq K
and

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
, then

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ,\Delta\vdash\!\mathcal{J}
.

The proofs of both lemmas are routine inductions on the derivations of
the respective judgments. The proof of context narrowing is only easy
thanks to the rule
CV-Sub
. Without this rule, the canonical
kinding judgments for variables and neutral types would become
synthesis judgments and context narrowing would no longer hold in its
present form for these two judgments. To see this, consider the
variable kinding judgment
\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash_{\sf var}X:K
,
which, after eliminating
CV-Sub
, could only be derived
using
CV-Var
. If we were to narrow the context by changing
the declared kind
K
of
X
to some
J
such that

\Gamma\vdash J\leq K
, then the synthesized kind of
X
would
necessarily change to
J
too.

For neutral kinding, we would be in a similar situation as the new
synthesized kind of the head would have to be propagated through the
kinding derivation of the spine. Along the way, the new kind
J

would have to be unraveled by repeatedly separating

J=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}})\rightarrow{J_{2}}
into
J_{1}
,
J_{2}
and hereditarily
substituting the next element of the spine into the codomain
J_{2}
.
To prove context narrowing for the remainder of the canonical
judgments, we would have to maintain the invariant that the new
synthesized kind of a neutral type is a subtype of the original one,
i.e. if
\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1},\Delta\vdash_{\sf var}N\,\rightrightarrows\,K_{2}
and

\Gamma\vdash J_{1}\leq K_{1}
then

\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ_{1},\Delta\vdash_{\sf var}N\,\rightrightarrows\,J_{2}
and

\Gamma\vdash J_{2}\leq K_{2}
. Because spine kinding involves hereditary
substitution, a proof involving this invariant would require a
hereditary substitution lemma for subtyping, i.e. a proof that
hereditary substitutions preserve subtyping. We give such a proof in

§
D.2
and, as we will see there, it makes crucial use of
context narrowing itself. By allowing us to establish

Lemma
D.8
independently, the rule
CV-Sub
thus
simplifies the proof of an otherwise rather complicated lemma (see

Lemma
D.17
for details).

D.1.1.
Order-Theoretic Properties

Having established context narrowing, we can prove the usual
order-theoretic properties of canonical subkinding, subtyping, as well
as kind and type equality. We start by stating an proving the various
reflexivity properties which, unlike those for the declarative
relations, have to be proven simultaneously for the canonical
variants.

Lemma D.9.

The following reflexivity rules are all admissible.

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash K\leq K\end{array}}

(
CSK-Refl
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\,\rightrightarrows\,V\intv W\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\leq U\end{array}}

(
CST-ReflSyn
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash K=K\end{array}}

(
CKEq-Refl
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,K\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash V\leq V\,\leftleftarrows\,K\end{array}}

(
CST-ReflCk
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash V\,\rightrightarrows\,J\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash J\leq K\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash V\leq V\,\leftleftarrows\,K\end{array}}

(
CST-ReflSub
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash J\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}\,\rightrightarrows\,K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash J\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}\,\rightrightarrows\,K\end{array}}

(
SpEq-Refl
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,K\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash V=V\,\leftleftarrows\,K\end{array}}

(
CTEq-Refl
)

Proof.

The proof is by mutual induction in the structure of the kinds and
types being related to themselves, and then by case-analysis on the
final rules of the corresponding kind formation and kinding
derivations. The proof for
CST-ReflSub
proceeds by an
inner induction on the derivation of
\Gamma\vdash J\leq K
. In the
case for
CSK-DArr
where
V=\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}}.\,U
and we have

\Gamma\vdash K_{1}\leq J_{1}
,
\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1}\vdash J_{2}\leq K_{2}

and
\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ_{1}\vdash U\,\rightrightarrows\,J_{2}
, we use context narrowing to
adjust the declared kind of
X
from
J_{1}
to
K_{1}
in

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ_{1}\vdash U\,\rightrightarrows\,J_{2}
before applying the IH to
obtain
\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1}\vdash U\leq U\,\leftleftarrows\,K_{2}
.
∎

Transitivity of the various relations and symmetry of the equalities
are more easily established, thanks to the rule
CST-Trans
on
the one hand, and to the structure of equality on the other.

Lemma D.10.

Canonical subkinding, subtyping, kind and type equality are
transitive.

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash J\leq K\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash K\leq L\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash J\leq L\end{array}}

(
CSK-Trans
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash J=K\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash K=L\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash J=L\end{array}}

(
CKEq-Trans
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\leq V\,\leftleftarrows\,K\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash V\leq W\,\leftleftarrows\,K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\leq W\,\leftleftarrows\,K\end{array}}

(
CST-TransCk
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U=V\,\leftleftarrows\,K\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash V=W\,\leftleftarrows\,K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U=W\,\leftleftarrows\,K\end{array}}

(
CTEq-Trans
)

Proof.

The proof of
CSK-Trans
is by induction on the structure of

K
and case analysis on the final rules used to derive the
premises. In the case for
K=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}
we use context
narrowing. The proof of
CST-Trans
is by induction on the
derivation of the first premise. The proofs of
CKEq-Trans

and
CTEq-Trans
are by inspection of the equality rules and
use
CSK-Trans
and
CST-Trans
, respectively.
∎

Lemma D.11.

Canonical kind and type equality are symmetric.

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash J=K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash K=J\end{array}}

(
CKEq-Sym
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U=V\,\leftleftarrows\,K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash V=U\,\leftleftarrows\,K\end{array}}

(
CTEq-Sym
)

Proof.

By inspection of the equality rules,
CSK-AntiSym

and
CST-AntiSym
.
∎

Thanks to context narrowing and subkinding transitivity, we can prove
admissibility of the following subsumption rules for the three checked
judgments.

Lemma D.12.

Kind subsumption is admissible in the checked judgments.

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\,\leftleftarrows\,J\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash J\leq K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\,\leftleftarrows\,K\end{array}}

(CK-SubCk)

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\leq V\,\leftleftarrows\,J\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash J\leq K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\leq V\,\leftleftarrows\,K\end{array}}
(CST-SubCk)

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U=V\,\leftleftarrows\,J\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash J\leq K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U=V\,\leftleftarrows\,K\end{array}}
(CTEq-SubCk)

Proof.

Admissibility is proven separately for the three rules, in the order
they are listed. The proof of
CK-SubCk
is by inspection
of the kind checking rules
and uses
CSK-Trans
. The proof of
CST-SubCk
is
by induction on the derivation of the second premise

\Gamma\vdash J\leq K
and uses
CK-SubCk
as well as context
narrowing for the inductive step. The proof of
CTEq-SubCk

is by inspection of the equality rules and uses
CST-SubCk
.
∎

Kind subsumption subsumes kind conversion thanks to the first of the
following three rules, all of which follow immediately by inspection
of the equality and checked subtyping rules.

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash J=K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash J\leq K\end{array}}

(CSK-Refl-KEq)

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U=V\,\leftleftarrows\,K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\leq V\,\leftleftarrows\,K\end{array}}
(CST-Refl-TEq)

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U=V\,\leftleftarrows\,W\intv W^{\prime}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\leq V\end{array}}
(CST-Refl-TEq’)

D.1.2.
Canonical Replacements for Declarative Rules

Our completeness proof for the canonical system relies on the fact
that the normal form
\mathsf{nf}(A)
of any declaratively well-kinded type

\Gamma\vdash A:K
kind checks against the normal form
\mathsf{nf}(K)

of the corresponding kind
K
, i.e. that we have

\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\vdash\mathsf{nf}(A)\,\leftleftarrows\,\mathsf{nf}(K)
. To simplify the proof of
this fact, we establish a set of admissible kind checking rules below
that mirror the corresponding declarative rules.

We begin by proving that normal forms with synthesized or checked
interval kinds also check against
{*}
.

Lemma D.13.

Types inhabiting interval kinds are proper types. If

\;\Gamma\vdash U\,\rightrightarrows\,V\intv W
or

\;\Gamma\vdash U\,\leftleftarrows\,V\intv W
, then also

\Gamma\vdash U\,\leftleftarrows\,{*}
.

Proof.

If
\;\Gamma\vdash U\,\rightrightarrows\,V\intv W
, then by
Lemma
D.3
,

\;\Gamma\vdash U\,\rightrightarrows\,U\intv U
. From this we derive the result
by
CST-Bot
,
CST-Top
,
CSK-Intv

and
CK-Sub
. If
\;\Gamma\vdash U\,\leftleftarrows\,V\intv W
, then
this must have been derived using
CK-Sub
and hence

\;\Gamma\vdash U\,\rightrightarrows\,K
and
\Gamma\vdash K\leq V\intv W
. By
inspection of the subkinding rules,
K=V^{\prime}\intv W^{\prime}
for some
V^{\prime}

and
W^{\prime}
. The result then follows by the first part of the lemma.
∎

The second part of the proof follows a pattern that is is quite
typical for proofs in the remainder of this section. Thanks to the
division of kinding into kind synthesis and checking, and thanks to
the simplicity of both kind checking and subkinding derivations, we
can often prove properties of kind checking judgments

\Gamma\vdash U\,\leftleftarrows\,K
by appealing to similar properties of kind
synthesis judgments
\Gamma\vdash U\,\rightrightarrows\,K^{\prime}
where
K
and
K^{\prime}
have the
same shape, i.e. where
K
and
K^{\prime}
are either both intervals or both
arrows. Two more examples of this pattern appear in the following
lemma, where the admissibility proofs of the
rules
CST-CkBnd
1

and
CST-CkBnd
2
, which have kind checking
judgments as their premises, appeal to instances
of
CST-SynBnd
1

and
CST-SynBnd
2
, respectively, which have
similar kind synthesis judgments as their premises.

Lemma D.14.

All of the following are admissible.

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\,\leftleftarrows\,V\intv W\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\,\rightrightarrows\,U\intv U\end{array}}

(
CK-Sing’
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash V\,\rightrightarrows\,K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,K\end{array}}

(
CK-SynCk
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}N:U\intv V\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash N\,\leftleftarrows\,U\intv V\end{array}}

(
CK-NeCk
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\,\leftleftarrows\,{*}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,{*}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\rightarrow V\,\leftleftarrows\,{*}\end{array}}

(
CK-Arr’
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}\quad\quad\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,{*}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{V}\,\leftleftarrows\,{*}\end{array}}

(
CK-All’
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kd}}\quad\quad\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J}.\,V\,\leftleftarrows\,({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{K}\end{array}}

(
CK-Abs’
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash V_{1}\leq V_{2}\,\leftleftarrows\,U\intv W\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash V_{1}\leq V_{2}\,\leftleftarrows\,V_{1}\intv V_{2}\end{array}}

(
CST-Intv’
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\,\rightrightarrows\,V_{1}\intv V_{2}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash V_{1}\leq U\end{array}}

(
CST-SynBnd
1
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\,\leftleftarrows\,V_{1}\intv V_{2}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash V_{1}\leq U\end{array}}

(
CST-CkBnd
1
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\,\rightrightarrows\,V_{1}\intv V_{2}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\leq V_{2}\end{array}}

(
CST-SynBnd
2
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\,\leftleftarrows\,V_{1}\intv V_{2}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash U\leq V_{2}\end{array}}

(
CST-CkBnd
2
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash J\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}\,\rightrightarrows\,({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K})\rightarrow{L}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,K\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash J\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}},V\,\rightrightarrows\,L[V/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\end{array}}

(
CK-Snoc
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash J\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{1}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{2}\,\rightrightarrows\,({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K})\rightarrow{L}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash V_{1}=V_{2}\,\leftleftarrows\,K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash J\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{1},V_{1}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{2},V_{2}\,\rightrightarrows\,L[V_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\end{array}}

(
SpEq-Snoc
)

Proof.

Some of the rules are derivable others are admissible; most of the
proofs are straightforward, so we omit the details. The proofs of
the alternate type formation rules use
CK-Sing’
and

Lemma
D.13
. The proofs of the alternate projection
rules
CST-SynBnd
1

and
CST-SynBnd
2
use
Lemma
D.3

and reflexivity; those of
CST-CkBnd
1

and
CST-CkBnd
2
are by inspection of kind
checking and subkinding and
use
CST-SynBnd
1

and
CST-SynBnd
2
, respectively. The
proofs of the last two rules are by induction on the derivations of
the respective first premises.
∎

As in the declarative system, we define
canonical context
equality

{\Gamma=\Delta\;\mathsf{ctx}}
as the pointwise lifting of
canonical type and kind equality to context bindings:

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\varnothing=\varnothing\;\mathsf{ctx}}\end{array}}

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\Gamma=\Delta\;\mathsf{ctx}}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash J=K\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ=\Delta,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK\;\mathsf{ctx}}\end{array}}

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\Gamma=\Delta\;\mathsf{ctx}}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash U=V\,\leftleftarrows\,W\intv W^{\prime}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\Gamma,x\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muU=\Delta,x\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muV\;\mathsf{ctx}}\end{array}}

D.1.3.
Simplification of Canonical Kinding

In
§
C.2.2
of the previous section, we showed that every
well-formed kind and well-kinded type has a simply well-formed or
simply well-kinded normal form, respectively (see
Lemma
C.14
).
Canonically well-formed kinds and canonically well-kinded types are
already in normal form, but we can still simplify their kind formation
and kinding derivations, as the following pair of lemma shows. In the
statement of the second lemma, we use the subscript “
\mathsf{nes}
”
to mark simple kinding judgments for neutral types and
“
\mathsf{ne}
” to mark their canonical counterparts.

Lemma D.15.

Canonical subkinds and equal kinds simplify equally. If

\;\Gamma\vdash J\leq K
or
\;\Gamma\vdash J=K
then

\lvert J\rvert\equiv\lvert K\rvert
.

Proof.

Separately, by induction on subkinding and kind equality
derivations, respectively.
∎

Lemma D.16 (simplification).

Well-formed kinds and well-kinded normal forms, neutrals and spines
are also simply well-formed and well-kinded, respectively.

(1)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf var}X:K
, then

\;\lvert\Gamma\rvert\vdash_{\sf nes}X:\lvert K\rvert
.

(2)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
, then

\;\lvert\Gamma\rvert\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kds}}
.

(3)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}N:K
, then

\;\lvert\Gamma\rvert\vdash_{\sf nes}N:\lvert K\rvert
.

(4)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash V\,\rightrightarrows\,K
, then

\;\lvert\Gamma\rvert\vdash V:\lvert K\rvert
.

(5)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,K
, then

\;\lvert\Gamma\rvert\vdash V:\lvert K\rvert
.

(6)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash J\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}\,\rightrightarrows\,K
,
then
\;\lvert\Gamma\rvert\vdash\lvert J\rvert:\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}:\lvert K\rvert
.

Proof.

Part
1
is proven separately,
parts
2
–
6
are proven
simultaneously, all by induction on the derivations of the
respective premises. The cases of
CV-Sub

and
CK-Sub
use of
Lemma
D.15
.
∎

Thanks to
Lemma
D.16
, properties of simply kinded normal forms
still hold for canonically kinded normal forms. For example,
by
Lemmas
D.16
and
C.11
, hereditary
substitutions in canonically kinded types commute.

D.2.
The Hereditary Substitution Lemma

We have arrived at
the core of the technical development of this section: the proof of
the
hereditary substitution lemma
. The hereditary substitution
lemma states, roughly, that canonical judgments are preserved by
hereditary substitutions of canonically well-kinded types. Just as
the ordinary substitution lemma for the declarative system
(
Lemma
B.4
)
played a key role in the proofs of several metatheoretic properties
in
§
3
of the paper,
the hereditary substitution lemma is key to
proving important metatheoretic properties of the canonical system.
But unlike that of its ordinary counterpart, the proof of the
hereditary substitution lemma is rather challenging. This is
reflected already in the statement of the lemma, which
features
24
separate parts, all of which have to be
proven simultaneously (see
Lemma
D.17
below).

One reason for the large number of parts is simply that there are more
judgment forms in the canonical system than there are in the
declarative system. But the foremost reason is that the proof of the
hereditary substitution lemma circularly depends on

functionality of the canonical judgments
, i.e. on the fact
that hereditarily substituting canonically equal types in normal forms
yields canonically equal normal forms.
This also renders the proof more challenging since both properties
have to be established at the same time.

The main source of complexity is the subtyping rule
CST-Ne
.
It is because of this rule that we have to prove the hereditary
substitution and functionality lemmas simultaneously.

To illustrate this, consider the neutral types
N={X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}

and
M={X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}}
with
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}=V_{1},V_{2}
,

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}=W_{1},W_{2}
such that

X\notin\mathsf{fv}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}})\cup\mathsf{fv}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}})
, and assume some
\Gamma
,

\Delta
and
U
such that

\displaystyle\Gamma,\Delta\;\vdash\;\lambda{Y_{1}}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}}.\,\lambda{Y_{2}}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{2}}.\,U\;\,\leftleftarrows\,\;J

and

\displaystyle\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ,\Delta\;\vdash\;J\;\,\rightrightarrows\,\;\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}\;=\;\boldsymbol{\mathrm{W}}\;\,\rightrightarrows\,\;{*}

for
J=({Y_{1}}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}})\rightarrow{({Y_{2}}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{2}})\rightarrow{J_{3}}}
. Then,
by
CST-Ne
, we have
\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ,\Delta\vdash N\leq M
.

We would like to hereditarily substitute

U^{\prime}=\lambda{Y_{1}}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}}.\,\lambda{Y_{2}}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{2}}.\,U
for
X
in
N
and
M
and
show that the resulting types remain subtypes, i.e. that

\Gamma,\Delta[U^{\prime}/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}]\;\vdash\;N[U^{\prime}/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}]\;\leq\;M[U^{\prime}/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}].

By the definition of hereditary substitution, we have

\displaystyle N[U^{\prime}/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}]\;

\displaystyle\equiv\;({X}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}})[U^{\prime}/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}]\;\equiv\;{{U^{\prime}}\,\cdot^{\lvert J\rvert}}\,{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}[U^{\prime}/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}])}

\displaystyle\equiv\;{{{{U^{\prime}}\,\cdot^{\lvert J\rvert}}\,{V_{1}}}\,\cdot^{\lvert J_{2}\rvert\rightarrow\lvert J_{3}\rvert}}\,{V_{2}}

\displaystyle\equiv\;{{((\lambda{Y_{2}}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{2}}.\,U)[V_{1}/{Y_{1}}^{\,\lvert J_{1}\rvert}])}\,\cdot^{\lvert J_{2}\rvert\rightarrow\lvert J_{3}\rvert}}\,{V_{2}}

\displaystyle\equiv\;{{(\lambda{Y_{2}}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{2}[V_{1}/{Y_{1}}^{\,\lvert J_{1}\rvert}]}.\,U[V_{1}/{Y_{1}}^{\,\lvert J_{1}\rvert}])}\,\cdot^{\lvert J_{2}\rvert\rightarrow\lvert J_{3}\rvert}}\,{V_{2}}

\displaystyle\equiv\;V^{\prime}[V_{2}/{Y_{2}}^{\,\lvert J_{2}\rvert}]

where
V^{\prime}=U[V_{1}/{Y_{1}}^{\,\lvert J_{1}\rvert}]
. Similarly,

M[U^{\prime}/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}]\equiv W^{\prime}[W_{2}/{Y_{2}}^{\,\lvert J_{2}\rvert}]

for
W^{\prime}=U[W_{1}/{Y_{1}}^{\,\lvert J_{1}\rvert}]
. Hence, we need to show
that

\displaystyle\Gamma,\Delta[U^{\prime}/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}]\;

\displaystyle\vdash\;{}

\displaystyle U\,[V_{1}/{Y_{1}}^{\,\lvert J_{1}\rvert}]

\displaystyle{}\;\leq\;{}

\displaystyle U\,[W_{1}/{Y_{1}}^{\,\lvert J_{1}\rvert}]

and

\displaystyle\Gamma,\Delta[U^{\prime}/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}]\;

\displaystyle\vdash\;{}

\displaystyle V^{\prime}[V_{2}/{Y_{2}}^{\,\lvert J_{2}\rvert}]

\displaystyle{}\;\leq\;{}

\displaystyle W^{\prime}[W_{2}/{Y_{2}}^{\,\lvert J_{2}\rvert}]

.

Since they belong to equal spines,
V_{1}
and
W_{1}
are judgmentally
equal as types, and so are
V_{2}
and
W_{2}
. But in general, neither
of these pairs of types are syntactically equal, i.e.
V_{1}\not\equiv W_{1}
,
V_{2}\not\equiv W_{2}
and
V^{\prime}\not\equiv W^{\prime}
. To
establish the above inequations, we therefore need to show that
simultaneous hereditary substitutions of judgmentally equal types
preserve inequations.

The example illustrates a second point, namely that, in order to prove
that hereditary substitutions preserve canonical kinding and
subtyping, we need to prove that kinding and subtyping of reducing
applications is admissible.
Our hereditary substitution lemma must cover all of these properties,
leading to the aforementioned grand total
of
24
parts.

Lemma D.17 (hereditary substitution).

Hereditary substitutions of canonically kind-checked types preserve
the canonical judgments; substitutions of canonically equal
types in canonically well-formed and well-kinded expressions result
in canonical equations; substitutions of canonically equal
types preserve canonical (in)equations; kinding and subtyping of
reducing applications is admissible. Assume that the following equations hold

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash U_{1}\;=\;U_{2}\,\leftleftarrows\,K

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\Sigma\;=\;\Gamma,\Delta[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\;\mathsf{ctx}}

\displaystyle{\Gamma,\Sigma\;=\;\Gamma,\Delta[U_{2}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\;\mathsf{ctx}}

for given
\Gamma
,
\Delta
,
\Sigma
,
U_{1}
,
U_{2}
,
K
and

X\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma,\Delta,\Sigma)
.

(1)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kd}}
, then

\;\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash{J[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\;\mathsf{kd}}
.

(2)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash_{\sf var}X:J
, then

\;\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash U_{1}\,\leftleftarrows\,J[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]
.

(3)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash_{\sf var}Y:J
and
Y\neq X
, then

\;\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash_{\sf var}Y:J[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]
.

(4)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash_{\sf ne}N:V\intv W
, then

\;\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash N[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\,\leftleftarrows\,(V\intv W)[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]
.

(5)

If
\;\lvert\Gamma\rvert\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kds}}

and
\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash J\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}\,\rightrightarrows\,L
, then

\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash J[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\,\rightrightarrows\,L[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}].

(6)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash V\,\rightrightarrows\,J
, then

\;\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash V[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\,\rightrightarrows\,J[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]
.

(7)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,J
, then

\;\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash V[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\,\leftleftarrows\,J[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]
.

(8)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kd}}
, then

\;\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash J[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\leq J[U_{2}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]
.

(9)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kd}}
, then

\;\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash J[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]=J[U_{2}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]
.

(10)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash_{\sf var}X:J
, then

\;\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash U_{1}=U_{2}\,\leftleftarrows\,J[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]
.

(11)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash_{\sf ne}N:V\intv W
, then

\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash N[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\leq N[U_{2}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]
.

(12)

If
\;\lvert\Gamma\rvert\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kds}}

and
\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash J\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}\,\rightrightarrows\,L
, then

\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash J[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}[U_{2}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\,\rightrightarrows\,L[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}].

(13)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash V\,\rightrightarrows\,W\intv W^{\prime}
, then

\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash V[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\leq V[U_{2}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]
.

(14)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash V\,\rightrightarrows\,J
and

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kd}}
, then

\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash V[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\leq V[U_{2}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\,\leftleftarrows\,J[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}].

(15)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,J
and

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kd}}
, then

\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash V[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\leq V[U_{2}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\,\leftleftarrows\,J[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}].

(16)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash J_{1}\leq J_{2}
, then

\;\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash J_{1}[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\leq J_{2}[U_{2}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]
.

(17)

If

\;\lvert\Gamma\rvert\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kds}}
and

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash J\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}_{1}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}_{2}\,\rightrightarrows\,L
,
then

\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash J[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}_{1}[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}_{2}[U_{2}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\,\rightrightarrows\,L[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}].

(18)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash V_{1}\leq V_{2}
, then

\;\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash V_{1}[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\leq V_{2}[U_{2}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]
.

(19)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash V_{1}\leq V_{2}\,\leftleftarrows\,J
and

\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kd}}
, then

\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash V_{1}[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\leq V_{2}[U_{2}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\,\leftleftarrows\,J[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}].

(20)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK,\Delta\vdash V_{1}=V_{2}\,\leftleftarrows\,J
, then

\;\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash V_{1}[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]=V_{2}[U_{2}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\,\leftleftarrows\,J[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]
.

(21)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash K\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}\,\rightrightarrows\,J
,
then
\;\Gamma\vdash{{U_{1}}\,\cdot^{\lvert K\rvert}}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}\,\leftleftarrows\,J
.

(22)

If
\;K=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}
,

\;\Gamma\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,K_{1}
and
\;\Gamma\vdash{K_{1}\;\mathsf{kd}}
, then

\;\Gamma\vdash{{U_{1}}\,\cdot^{\lvert K\rvert}}\,{V}\,\leftleftarrows\,K_{2}[V/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}]
.

(23)

If

\;\Gamma\vdash K\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}_{1}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}_{2}\,\rightrightarrows\,J
, then

\;\Gamma\vdash{{U_{1}}\,\cdot^{\lvert K\rvert}}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}_{1}}={{U_{2}}\,\cdot^{\lvert K\rvert}}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}_{2}}\,\leftleftarrows\,J
.

(24)

If
\;K=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}
and

\;\Gamma\vdash V_{1}=V_{2}\,\leftleftarrows\,K_{1}
, then

\Gamma\vdash{{U_{1}}\,\cdot^{\lvert K\rvert}}\,{V_{1}}={{U_{1}}\,\cdot^{\lvert K\rvert}}\,{V_{2}}\,\leftleftarrows\,K_{2}[V_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}].

Proof.

As for the proof of
Lemma
C.10
, the structure of the proof
mirrors that of the recursive definition of hereditary substitution
itself. All
24
parts are proven simultaneously by
induction in the structure of the simple kind
\lvert K\rvert
.
Parts
1
–
20
proceed by an
inner induction on the respective formation, kinding, subkinding,
subtyping or equality derivations of the expressions in which
U_{1}

and
U_{2}
are being substituted for
X
.
Parts
21
–
24
proceed by a case
analysis on the final rule used to derive

\;\Gamma\vdash K\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}\,\rightrightarrows\,J
,
\;\Gamma\vdash U_{1}\,\leftleftarrows\,K
,

\;\Gamma\vdash K\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}_{1}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}_{2}\,\rightrightarrows\,J
and

\;\Gamma\vdash U_{1}=U_{2}\,\leftleftarrows\,K
, respectively.

The proofs of parts
1
–
7
are
similar to that of the declarative substitution lemma
(
Lemma
B.4
), while those of
parts
8
–
20

resemble the proof of the extended functionality lemma
(
Lemma
B.14
). In cases like
CWf-DArr
or

CK-All
, where the context is extended by an additional
binding, we use the IH together with context narrowing
(
Lemma
D.8
) to maintain the invariants

\;{\Gamma,\Sigma\;=\;\Gamma,\Delta[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\;\mathsf{ctx}}

and

\;{\Gamma,\Sigma\;=\;\Gamma,\Delta[U_{2}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\;\mathsf{ctx}}
.

The cases where the proofs of
parts
1
–
20
differ most
substantially from those of
Lemma
B.4
and

Lemma
B.14
are
parts
4
,
11
and the case
for
CST-Ne
of part
18
, which deal
with neutral types.
There, we proceed by case distinction on
X=Y
, where
Y
is the
head of the corresponding neutral types. If
X=Y
, then we
proceed using either part
21
, or
part
23
followed by
CST-Refl-TEq’
. If

X\neq Y
, then we use part
3
and proceed
with either part
5
followed by
CK-NeCk
,
or with parts
12
or
17

followed by
CST-Ne
. In the cases
for
CST-Bnd
1

and
CST-Bnd
2
, we use
part
4
followed
by
CST-CkBnd
1

or
CST-CkBnd
1
.

In the cases for
CK-Cons
and
SpEq-Cons
of
parts
5
,
12

and
17
, respectively, where

J=({Y}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}})\rightarrow{J_{2}}
, we use

Lemma
C.11
.
1
to show that hereditary
substitutions in kinds commute, i.e. that

J_{2}[V_{1}/{Y}^{\,\lvert J_{1}\rvert}][U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]\equiv J_{2}[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}][V_{1}[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K\rvert}]/{Y}^{\,\lvert J_{1}\rvert}].

The necessary simple kinding derivations are provided by case
analysis of the final rule used to derive the premise

\;\lvert\Gamma\rvert\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kds}}
and

Lemma
D.16
.
5
.

The proofs of parts
22
and
24

resemble that of
Lemma
C.10
.
5
but are
complicated slightly by the presence of subkinding. We show the
proof of part
22
, that of part
24

is similar. We have
K=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}
,

\;\Gamma\vdash U_{1}\,\leftleftarrows\,K
,
\;\Gamma\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,K_{1}
and

\;\Gamma\vdash{K_{1}\;\mathsf{kd}}
, and we want to show that

\;\Gamma\vdash{{U_{1}}\,\cdot^{\lvert K\rvert}}\,{V}\,\leftleftarrows\,K_{2}[V/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}]
.
By inspection of the kind checking and subkinding rules, we must
have
U_{1}=\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}}.\,U
such that

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ_{1}\vdash U\,\rightrightarrows\,J_{2}
,

\;\Gamma\vdash K_{1}\leq J_{1}
and

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1}\vdash J_{2}\leq K_{2}
, and by the definition of
reducing application,

{{U_{1}}\,\cdot^{\lvert K\rvert}}\,{V}\equiv U[V/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}]
.
Using context narrowing and the IH for part
7
, we
obtain

\Gamma\vdash U[V/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}]\,\rightrightarrows\,J_{2}[V/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}]
.
By
TEq-Refl
and the IH for part
16
,
we have

\Gamma\vdash J_{2}[V/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}]\leq K_{2}[V/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}]
. We conclude by
CK-Sub
.
∎

D.2.1.
Validity

With the hereditary substitution lemma in place, we can now prove the
remaining validity properties of the canonical judgments. The most
intricate cases are those for spine kinding and equality, which is
where we use
Lemma
D.17
.

Lemma D.18 (canonical validity – part 2).

(spine kinding validity)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kd}}
and
\;\Gamma\vdash J\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}\,\rightrightarrows\,K
,
then
\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
.

(spine equation validity)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash J_{1}\leq J_{2}
and

\;\Gamma\vdash J_{2}\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}\,\rightrightarrows\,K_{2}
, then

\Gamma\vdash J_{2}\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}\,\rightrightarrows\,K_{2}
,
\;\Gamma\vdash J_{1}\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}\,\rightrightarrows\,K_{1}
and
\Gamma\vdash K_{1}\leq K_{2}

for some
K_{1}
.

(neutral kinding validity)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash N:K
, then
\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
.

(subkinding validity)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash J\leq K
, then
\Gamma\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kd}}
and

\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
.

(proper subtyping validity)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash U\leq V
, then
\Gamma\vdash U\,\rightrightarrows\,U\intv U
and

\Gamma\vdash V\,\rightrightarrows\,V\intv V
.

(checked kinding validity)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,K
, then
\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
.

Proof.

Subkinding and proper subtyping validity are proven simultaneously,
the remaining parts are proven separately, in the order they are
listed. All parts are proven by induction on derivations of the
judgments they are named after: spine kinding and equation validity
are proven by induction on their respective second premises, the
remaining parts on their respective first premises. In inductive
steps of the proofs of spine kinding and equation validity, we use
the hereditary substitution lemmas to derive suitable first premises
for applying the IH. The proof of spine equation validity relies on
checked equation validity from
Lemma
D.18
. The proof of
neutral kinding validity relies on spine kinding validity. In the
proof of proper subtyping validity, we use neutral kinding validity
in the cases for the bound projection
rules
CST-Bnd
1,2
. The proof of checked
kinding validity relies on proper subtyping validity.
∎

D.2.2.
Lifting of Weak Equality to Canonical Equality

In
§
C.2.2
of the previous section, we established a number of
weak commutativity properties (see
Lemmas
C.16

and
C.21
). Among others, we showed that
normalization weakly commutes with hereditary substitution. But up
until now, we do not have any effective means to put these properties
to use – we have yet to establish a relationship between weak
equality and the equality judgments of the declarative and canonical
systems.

To remedy this situation, we prove that a weak equation
U\approx V

can be lifted to canonical equation
\Gamma\vdash U=V\,\leftleftarrows\,K
,
provided the left- and right-hand sides
U
,
V
are well-kinded, i.e.
\Gamma\vdash U\,\leftleftarrows\,K
and
\Gamma\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,K
. Similarly, we show
that weakly equal kinds are canonically equal if they are well-formed.

Lemma D.19.

Weakly equal canonically well-formed kinds and well-kinded types are
canonically equal.

(1)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kd}}
,
\;\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
and
J\approx K
, then
\Gamma\vdash J\leq K
.

(2)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash U\,\rightrightarrows\,U\intv U
,
\;\Gamma\vdash V\,\rightrightarrows\,V\intv V
and
U\approx V
, then
\Gamma\vdash U\leq V
.

(3)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
,

\;\Gamma\vdash U\,\leftleftarrows\,K
,
\;\Gamma\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,K
and

U\approx V
, then
\Gamma\vdash U\leq V\,\leftleftarrows\,K
.

(4)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kd}}
,

\;\Gamma\vdash J\leq K_{1}
,
\;\Gamma\vdash J\leq K_{2}
,

\;\Gamma\vdash K_{1}\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{1}\,\rightrightarrows\,V_{1}\intv W_{1}
,

\;\Gamma\vdash K_{2}\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{2}\,\rightrightarrows\,V_{2}\intv W_{2}
, and

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{1}\approx\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{2}
, then

\Gamma\vdash J\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{1}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{2}\,\rightrightarrows\,V\intv W
for some

V
and
W
.

(5)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash{J\;\mathsf{kd}}
,
\;\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
and
J\approx K
, then
\Gamma\vdash J=K
.

(6)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
,

\;\Gamma\vdash U\,\leftleftarrows\,K
,
\;\Gamma\vdash V\,\leftleftarrows\,K
and

U\approx V
, then
\Gamma\vdash U=V\,\leftleftarrows\,K
.

Proof.

Simultaneously for all
6
parts by simultaneous
induction on the corresponding pairs of kinds, types or spines being
related, then by case analysis on the final rules used to derive the
corresponding formation, kinding and weak equality judgments. In
the proof of part
5
, we apply the IH for
part
1
directly to the equations
J\approx K
and

K\approx J
, where the latter is derived using symmetry of weak
equality. Neither
J
nor
K
decrease in this
step, but the proof of part
5
does not make any
further use of the IH and could therefore be inlined in the proofs
of the other parts. The proof of part
5
is
similar.

The proofs of the remaining parts are largely routine.
The most interesting case is
the inductive one in
part
4
, where we have

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{1}=(U_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{1}^{\prime})
,

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{2}=(U_{2},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{2}^{\prime})
,

K_{1}=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{11}})\rightarrow{K_{12}}
,
K_{2}=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{21}})\rightarrow{K_{22}}
,

\;U_{1}\approx U_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{1}^{\prime}\approx\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{2}^{\prime}
. Analyzing the
derivations of the remaining premises, we have

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash K_{11}

\displaystyle\leq J_{1}

\displaystyle\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{11}\vdash J_{2}

\displaystyle\leq K_{12}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash K_{21}

\displaystyle\leq J_{1}

\displaystyle\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{21}\vdash J_{2}

\displaystyle\leq K_{22}

such that
J=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}})\rightarrow{J_{2}}
, as well as

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash U_{1}

\displaystyle\,\leftleftarrows\,K_{11}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash K_{12}[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{11}\rvert}]\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{1}^{\prime}\,\rightrightarrows\,V_{1}\intv W_{1}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash U_{2}

\displaystyle\,\leftleftarrows\,K_{21}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash K_{22}[U_{2}/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{21}\rvert}]\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{U}}_{2}^{\prime}\,\rightrightarrows\,V_{2}\intv W_{2}

We use
CK-SubCk
and the IH for part
6
to
derive
\Gamma\vdash U_{1}=U_{2}\,\leftleftarrows\,J_{1}
, then we use hereditary
substitution (
Lemmas
D.17
.
16

and
D.17
.
8
) and

Lemma
D.15
to derive

\displaystyle\Gamma\;\vdash\;J_{2}[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert J_{1}\rvert}]\;

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle K_{12}[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{11}\rvert}]

\displaystyle\Gamma\;\vdash\;J_{2}[U_{1}/{X}^{\,\lvert J_{1}\rvert}]\;

\displaystyle\leq\;J_{2}[U_{2}/{X}^{\,\lvert J_{1}\rvert}]\;\leq\;

\displaystyle K_{22}[U_{2}/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{21}\rvert}]

We conclude the case by the IH for part
4

and
SpEq-Cons
.
∎

D.3.
Completeness of Canonical Kinding

In the previous section, we saw that every declaratively well-formed
kind or well-kinded type has a judgmentally equal
\beta\eta
-normal
form (
Lemmas
4.3
and
C.14
). In this section,
we prove that every declarative judgment has a canonical counterpart
where the expressions related by the original judgment have been
normalized. Roughly, whenever
\Gamma\vdash_{\sf d}\!\mathcal{J}
holds, we also have

\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\vdash_{\sf c}\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(\!\mathcal{J})
. Since
normalization does not change the meaning of an expression, this
result establishes completeness of the canonical system w.r.t. to the
declarative one.

There are several judgments for kinding types in the canonical system,
but only one in the declarative system. To establish completeness, we
show that, if a type
A
is of kind
K
according to declarative
kinding, then the normal form
\mathsf{nf}(A)
kind checks against the
normal form
\mathsf{nf}(K)
, i.e. if
\Gamma\vdash_{\sf d}A:K
, then

\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\vdash_{\sf c}\mathsf{nf}(A)\,\leftleftarrows\,\mathsf{nf}(K)
.

When
A
is a variable
A=X
, the normal form
\mathsf{nf}(A)
is its

\eta
-expansion
\mathsf{nf}(A)=\eta_{\mathsf{nf}(K)}(X)
and we use the
following lemma to prove that it kind checks against
\mathsf{nf}(K)
.

Lemma D.20 (
\eta
-expansion).

\eta
-expansion preserves the canonical kinds of neutral types. If

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}N:K
, then
\Gamma\vdash\eta_{K}(N)\,\leftleftarrows\,K
.

Instead of proving the lemma directly, we first prove the following
helper lemma.

Lemma D.21.

(1)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
and

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ\vdash\eta_{J}(X)\,\leftleftarrows\,J
, then

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ\vdash K[\eta_{J}(X)/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}]=K
.

(2)

If

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}N:J
and
\;\Gamma\vdash J\leq K
, then

\Gamma\vdash\eta_{K}(N)\,\leftleftarrows\,K
.

The first part says that hereditary substitutions of
\eta
-expanded
variables in kinds vanish, while the second part is a strengthened
version of
Lemma
D.20
.

Proof.

The two parts are proven separately. For the first part, we use
simplification of canonical kinding
(
Lemma
D.16
.
2
) and

Lemma
C.16
.
2
to derive

K[\eta_{J}(X)/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}]\approx K
. By weakening and
the hereditary substitution lemma
(
Lemma
D.17
.
1
), we have

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ\vdash{K[\eta_{J}(X)/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}]\;\mathsf{kd}}
.
The conclusion of the first part then follows by

Lemma
D.19
.
5
.

The proof of the second part is by induction on the structure of
K

and case analysis on the final rule used to derive

\Gamma\vdash J\leq K
. In the base case, we use
CK-NeCk

and
CK-SubCk
. In the inductive case, we have

K=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}
,
J=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}})\rightarrow{J_{2}}
such that

\Gamma\vdash K_{1}\leq J_{1}
and

\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1}\vdash J_{2}\leq K_{2}
. By subkinding validity, we
further have
\Gamma\vdash{K_{1}\;\mathsf{kd}}
and

\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1}\vdash{K_{2}\;\mathsf{kd}}
. By weakening, the IH
and
CSK-Refl
, we obtain first

\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1}\vdash\eta_{K_{1}}(X)\,\leftleftarrows\,K_{1}
, then

\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1}\vdash\eta_{K_{1}}(X)\,\leftleftarrows\,J_{1}
by weakening and

CK-SubCk
. By inspection of neutral kinding, we know that

N={Y}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}
for some
Y
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}
and that

\Gamma\vdash_{\sf var}Y:L
and

\Gamma\vdash L\,\rightrightarrows\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}\,\rightrightarrows\,({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}})\rightarrow{J_{2}}
. We use weakening,

CK-Snoc
and
CK-Ne
to derive

\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1}\vdash_{\sf ne}{{Y}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{V}}}}\,{(\eta_{K_{1}}(X))}:J_{2}[\eta_{K_{1}}(X)/{X}^{\,\lvert J_{1}\rvert}]
.

Now we see why it was necessary to strengthen the IH: the body of
the
\eta
-expansion of
N
has kind

J_{2}[\eta_{K_{1}}(X)/{X}^{\,\lvert J_{1}\rvert}]
rather than
K_{2}
as
required by
Lemma
D.20
. In order to apply the IH, we show
that

(by
Lemma
D.15
)

\displaystyle\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1}\;\vdash\;J_{2}[\eta_{K_{1}}(X)/{X}^{\,\lvert J_{1}\rvert}]\;

\displaystyle\equiv\;J_{2}[\eta_{K_{1}}(X)/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}]

(by
Lemma
D.17
.
16
)

\displaystyle\leq\;K_{2}[\eta_{K_{1}}(X)/{X}^{\,\lvert K_{1}\rvert}]

(by part
1
)

\displaystyle=\;K_{2}.

We conclude the case by the IH and
CK-Abs’
.
∎

Lemma
D.20
as well as a strengthened version of

Lemma
D.21
.
1
now follow as
corollaries.

Corollary D.22.

If
\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
, then

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ\vdash K[\eta_{J}(X)/{X}^{\,\lvert J\rvert}]=K
.

To establish completeness of the canonical system w.r.t. the
declarative system, we show that every declarative judgment derived
using the
extended
declarative rules, rather than the original
ones, has a canonical counterpart. The proof makes crucial use of the
validity conditions present in the extended rules.
To avoid confusion, we again mark canonical judgments with the
subscript “
\mathsf{c}
” and extended declarative ones with
“
\mathsf{e}
”. To enhance readability, we omit the subscript

\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)
, writing e.g.
\mathsf{nf}(A)
instead of

\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(A)
.

Lemma D.23 (completeness of the canonical rules – extended
version).

(1)

If
\;{\Gamma\;\mathsf{ctx}_{\sf e}}
, then

\;{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\;\mathsf{ctx}_{\sf c}}
.

(2)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf e}{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
, then

\;\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\vdash_{\sf c}{\mathsf{nf}(K)\;\mathsf{kd}}
.

(3)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf e}A:K
, then

\;\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\vdash_{\sf c}\mathsf{nf}(A)\,\leftleftarrows\,\mathsf{nf}(K)
.

(4)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf e}J\leq K
, then

\;\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\vdash_{\sf c}\mathsf{nf}(J)\leq\mathsf{nf}(K)
.

(5)

If

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf e}A\leq B:C\intv D
, then

\;\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\vdash_{\sf c}\mathsf{nf}(A)\leq\mathsf{nf}(B)
.

(6)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf e}A\leq B:K
, then

\;\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\vdash_{\sf c}\mathsf{nf}(A)\leq\mathsf{nf}(B)\,\leftleftarrows\,\mathsf{nf}(K)
.

(7)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf e}J=K
, then

\;\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\vdash_{\sf c}\mathsf{nf}(J)=\mathsf{nf}(K)
.

(8)

If
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf e}A=B:K
, then

\;\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\vdash_{\sf c}\mathsf{nf}(A)=\mathsf{nf}(B)\,\leftleftarrows\,\mathsf{nf}(K)
.

(9)

If

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf e}A:({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{K}
and
\;X\notin\mathsf{fv}(A)
, then

\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\vdash_{\sf c}\mathsf{nf}(\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J}.\,{A}\,{X})=\mathsf{nf}(A)\,\leftleftarrows\,\mathsf{nf}(({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{K}).

(10)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ\vdash_{\sf e}{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
,
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf e}A:J
and

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf e}{K[A/X]\;\mathsf{kd}}
, then

\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\vdash_{\sf c}\mathsf{nf}(K)[\mathsf{nf}(A)/{X}^{\,\lvert\mathsf{nf}(J)\rvert}]=\mathsf{nf}(K[A/X]).

(11)

If

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ\vdash_{\sf e}A:K
,
\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf e}B:J
,

\;\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ\vdash_{\sf e}{K\;\mathsf{kd}}
,

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf e}A[B/X]:K[B/X]

and

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf e}{K[B/X]\;\mathsf{kd}}
, then

\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)\vdash_{\sf c}\mathsf{nf}({(\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J}.\,A)}\,{B})=\mathsf{nf}(A[B/X])\,\leftleftarrows\,\mathsf{nf}(K[B/X]).

Equivalent statements w.r.t. the original declarative rules follow by
equivalence of the original and extended declarative systems.

Proof.

All parts are proven simultaneously, by induction on the derivations
of the respective premises, except for
parts
9
–
11
, which are helper
lemmas that apply the IH directly to all of their premises but could
be inlined in the proofs of the other parts.

Thanks to the admissible rules introduced in
Lemma
D.14
,
the proofs of parts
1
–
3
are
straightforward, except for the cases of
K-Var
, where we
use
Lemma
D.20
, and
K-App
, where we use the
hereditary substitution lemma to normalize
A={A_{1}}\,{A_{2}}
if

\mathsf{nf}(A_{1})
is an abstraction, and the IH for
part
10
together with
CK-SubCk
to
adjust the kind of the result. The validity conditions
of
K-App
are crucial in this last step.

Parts
5
and
8
follow almost
immediately from part
6
, part
7

from part
4
.

The proofs of parts
9
–
11
all
follow the same pattern. First, we use the IH to normalize the
premises and establish validity of the left- and right-hand sides of
the respective equations.
Then we use
Lemma
C.22
,

Lemma
C.21
.
1
and

Lemma
C.21
.
2
, respectively, to derive
weak versions of these equations, and
Lemma
D.19
to turn them
into canonical equations.

The remaining parts
4
and
6
are the
most difficult to prove. The cases of the extended
\beta
and

\eta
-conversion rules are covered by parts
9

and
11
thanks to the validity conditions in the
extended rules. The case of
ST-App
is similar to that
of
K-App
– again the validity conditions are crucial.
Some of the remaining cases are covered by the admissible rules
introduced in

§§
D.1.1
and
D.1.2
.
The challenging cases are those where one of the premises of the
corresponding rule extends the contexts, i.e. those
of
CSK-DArr
,
CST-All
and
CST-Abs
.
We show the case for
CSK-DArr
here, the other two are
similar.

We are given
\Gamma\vdash_{\sf e}{({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}})\rightarrow{J_{2}}\;\mathsf{kd}}
,

\;\Gamma\vdash_{\sf e}K_{1}\leq J_{1}
and

\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1}\vdash_{\sf e}J_{2}\leq K_{2}

with
J=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J_{1}})\rightarrow{J_{2}}

and
K=({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}})\rightarrow{K_{2}}
. We start by applying the IH to all
the premises and analyze the first of the resulting derivations to
obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle\vdash_{\sf c}{\mathsf{nf}(J_{1})\;\mathsf{kd}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma),X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\mathsf{nf}(J_{1})

\displaystyle\vdash_{\sf c}{\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ_{1})}(J_{2})\;\mathsf{kd}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle\vdash_{\sf c}\mathsf{nf}(K_{1})\leq\mathsf{nf}(J_{1})

\displaystyle\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma),X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\mathsf{nf}(K_{1})

\displaystyle\vdash_{\sf c}\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1})}(J_{2})\leq\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1})}(K_{2}).

Note the different contexts
\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ_{1})
and

\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1})
used to normalize
J_{2}
in the second
and fourth of these judgments, respectively. This leads to a
syntactic difference in the resulting normal forms, i.e. we have

\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ_{1})}(J_{2})\not\equiv\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1})}(J_{2})
.
In order to apply
CSK-DArr
, we need to resolve this
difference.

We notice that

\lvert\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ_{1})\rvert\equiv\lvert\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1})\rvert

by
Lemma
D.15
. Hence, by
Lemma
C.6
, we have

\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ_{1})}(J_{2})\approx\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1})}(J_{2})
.
Using context narrowing and subkinding validity, we derive

\displaystyle\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma),X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\mathsf{nf}(K_{1})\vdash{\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ_{1})}(J_{2})\;\mathsf{kd}}

and

\displaystyle\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma),X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\mathsf{nf}(K_{1})\vdash{\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1})}(J_{2})\;\mathsf{kd}},

from which we obtain, by
Lemma
D.19
.
1
,

\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma),X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu\mathsf{nf}(K_{1})\;\vdash_{\sf c}\;\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muJ_{1})}(J_{2})\;=\;\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1})}(J_{2})\;\leq\;\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{1})}(K_{2})

We conclude the case by
CWf-DArr
and
CSK-DArr
.
∎

D.4.
Inversion of Subtyping

As we saw in
§
3
of the paper, preservation of types under CBV
reduction does not hold in arbitrary contexts. The culprit are type
variable bindings with inconsistent bounds. Such bindings can inject
arbitrary inequations into the subtyping relation and hence break
putative properties that hold for subtyping of closed types. For
example, the absurd assumption
X:\top\intv\bot
trivializes
the subtyping relation under any context in which it appears. To see
this, consider the following derivation, where

\Gamma=X:\top\intv\bot
.

\vdots

\Gamma\vdash U\rightarrow V\leq\top

\vdots

\Gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}X:\top\intv\bot

(
CST-Bnd
1
)

\Gamma\vdash\top\leq X

\vdots

\Gamma\vdash_{\sf ne}X:\top\intv\bot

(
CST-Bnd
2
)

\Gamma\vdash X\leq\bot

(
CST-Trans
)

\Gamma\vdash\top\leq\bot

(
CST-Trans
)

\Gamma\vdash U\rightarrow V\leq\bot

\vdots

\Gamma\vdash\bot\leq\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{W}

(
CST-Trans
)

\Gamma\vdash U\rightarrow V\leq\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{W}

Under such conditions, subtyping cannot be inverted in any meaningful
way.
We therefore consider inversion of canonical subtyping only in the
empty context, following the approach taken by Rompf and Amin in their
type safety proof for DOT
(
Rompf and Amin 2016
)

Transitivity-free subtyping of closed proper types

\vdash_{\sf tf}U\leq V

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\varnothing\vdash V\,\rightrightarrows\,V\intv V\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vdash_{\sf tf}V\leq\top\end{array}}

(
TfST-Top
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\
\varnothing\vdash U_{2}\leq U_{1}\quad\quad\varnothing\vdash V_{1}\leq V_{2}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vdash_{\sf tf}U_{1}\rightarrow V_{1}\leq U_{2}\rightarrow V_{2}\\
\end{array}}

(
TfST-Arr
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\varnothing\vdash V\,\rightrightarrows\,V\intv V\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vdash_{\sf tf}\bot\leq V\end{array}}

(
TfST-Bot
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\varnothing\vdash\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}}.\,{V_{1}}\,\rightrightarrows\,\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}}.\,{V_{1}}\intv\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}}.\,{V_{1}}\\
\varnothing\vdash K_{2}\leq K_{1}\quad\quad X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{2}\vdash V_{1}\leq V_{2}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vdash_{\sf tf}\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}}.\,{V_{1}}\leq\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{2}}.\,{V_{2}}\end{array}}

(
TfST-All
)

Figure 11
.
Top-level transitivity-free canonical subtyping
Top-level transitivity-free canonical subtyping

As a first step we show that any top-level uses of the transitivity
rule
CST-Trans
can be eliminated. To do so, we introduce a
helper judgment
\vdash_{\sf tf}U\leq V
, which states that
U
is a proper
subtype of
V
in the empty context (see
Fig.
11
). It
is easy to see that this judgment is sound w.r.t. canonical subtyping
in the empty context (the proof is by routine induction on subtyping
derivations).

Lemma D.24 (soundness of top-level subtyping).

If
\;\vdash_{\sf tf}U\leq V
, then
\varnothing\vdash U\leq V
.

Crucially, the inference rules for the judgment
\vdash_{\sf tf}U\leq V
do
not include a transitivity rule, but the following variant of that
rule is admissible.

Lemma D.25 (top-level transitivity elimination).

The following is admissible.

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\varnothing\vdash U\leq V\quad\quad\vdash_{\sf tf}V\leq W\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vdash_{\sf tf}U\leq W\end{array}}

(TfST-Trans)

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the derivation of the first premise and
case analysis of the final rule used to derive the second. In the
case of
CST-Trans
, we use the IH twice. In the case
of
CST-Bot
where
\varnothing\vdash\bot\leq U
and

\vdash_{\sf tf}U\leq V
, we use
Lemma
D.24
and validity of
canonical typing to derive
\varnothing\vdash V\,\rightrightarrows\,V\intv V
and conclude
with
TfST-Bot
. Similarly, in cases where the second
premise was derived using
TfST-Top
, we use validity of
canonical subtyping and
TfST-Top
.
∎

Thanks to
TfST-Trans
, it is straightforward to establish
completeness, and thus equivalence of the judgments

\varnothing\vdash U\leq V
and
\vdash_{\sf tf}U\leq V
.

Lemma D.26 (equivalence of top-level subtyping).

The two versions of canonical subtyping are equivalent in the empty
context:
\varnothing\vdash U\leq V
iff
\;\vdash_{\sf tf}U\leq V
.

Proof.

We have already proven soundness (
\Leftarrow
).
Completeness (
\Rightarrow
) is by induction on the derivations of

\varnothing\vdash U\leq V
and uses
TfST-Trans
in the case
of
CST-Trans
.
∎

Inversion of the canonical subtyping relation in the empty context now
follows immediately by inspection of the transitivity-free subtyping
rules and

Lemma
D.26
. We only state the relevant cases.

Corollary D.27 (inversion of canonical subtyping – embedding).

Let
\;\varnothing\vdash U_{1}\leq U_{2}
.

(1)

If
\;U_{1}=V_{1}\rightarrow W_{1}
and

U_{2}=V_{2}\rightarrow W_{2}
, then
\varnothing\vdash V_{2}\leq V_{1}
and

\varnothing\vdash W_{1}\leq W_{2}
.

(2)

If
\;U_{1}=\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}}.\,{V_{1}}
and

U_{2}=\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{2}}.\,{V_{2}}
, then
\varnothing\vdash K_{2}\leq K_{1}
and

X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{2}\vdash V_{1}\leq V_{2}
.

Corollary D.28 (inversion of canonical subtyping – contradiction).

For any
U
,
V
,
W
and
K
,

(1)

\varnothing\nvdash\top\leq\bot
,

(2)

\varnothing\nvdash U\rightarrow V\leq\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{W}
, and

(3)

\varnothing\nvdash\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{U}\leq V\rightarrow W
.

A bit more work is needed to also prove the declarative version of
subtyping inversion.
Again, we only state the relevant cases.

Lemma D.29 (inversion of declarative subtyping –
embedding).

Let
\;\varnothing\vdash A_{1}\leq A_{2}:{*}
.

(1)

If
\;A_{1}=B_{1}\rightarrow C_{1}
and

A_{2}=B_{2}\rightarrow C_{2}
, then
\varnothing\vdash B_{2}\leq B_{1}:{*}
and

\varnothing\vdash C_{1}\leq C_{2}:{*}
.

(2)

If
\;A_{1}=\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{1}}.\,{B_{1}}
and

A_{2}=\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K_{2}}.\,{B_{2}}
, then
\varnothing\vdash K_{2}\leq K_{1}
and

X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK_{2}\vdash B_{1}\leq B_{2}:{*}
.

The proof makes use of the following generation lemma for well-kinded
arrow and universal types, which is proven by induction on kinding
derivations.

Lemma D.30 (generation of kinding for arrows and universals).

The following are admissible.

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A\rightarrow B:{*}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash A:{*}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash B:{*}\end{array}}

\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{A}:{*}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash{K\;\mathsf{kd}}\quad\quad\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK\vdash A:{*}\end{array}}

Proof of
Lemma
D.29
.

By completeness of canonical subtyping and soundness of
normalization. We show only the first part, the second is
analogous. Assume

\varnothing\vdash B_{1}\rightarrow C_{1}\leq B_{2}\rightarrow C_{2}:{*}
. Then by validity
of declarative subtyping (
Lemma
3.1
), generation of
kinding for arrow types, soundness of normalization (
Lemma
4.3
)
and completeness of canonical subtyping, we have

\displaystyle\varnothing\vdash_{\sf d}B_{1}=\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(B_{1}):{*}\qquad\qquad\varnothing\vdash_{\sf d}C_{1}=\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(C_{1}):{*}

\displaystyle\varnothing\vdash_{\sf d}B_{2}=\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(B_{2}):{*}\qquad\qquad\varnothing\vdash_{\sf d}C_{2}=\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(C_{2}):{*}

\displaystyle\varnothing\vdash_{\sf c}\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(B_{1})\rightarrow\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(C_{1})\leq\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(B_{2})\rightarrow\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(C_{2})

By inversion and soundness of canonical subtyping, it follows that

\displaystyle\varnothing\vdash_{\sf d}\;B_{2}\;=\;\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(B_{2})\;\leq\;\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(B_{1})\;=\;B_{1}\;:\;{*}\quad\text{ and}

\displaystyle\varnothing\vdash_{\sf d}\;C_{1}\;=\;\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(C_{1})\;\leq\;\mathsf{nf}_{\mathsf{nf}(\Gamma)}(C_{2})\;=\;C_{2}\;:\;{*}.\qed

We also prove a declarative counterpart of
Corollary
D.28
, which is
used in the proof of the progress theorem below.

Lemma D.31 (inversion of declarative subtyping –
contradiction).

For any
A
,
B
,
C
and
K
,

(1)

\varnothing\nvdash\top\leq\bot
,

(2)

\varnothing\nvdash A\rightarrow B\leq\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{C}
, and

(3)

\varnothing\nvdash\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{A}\leq B\rightarrow C
.

Proof.

By completeness of canonical subtyping, then by contradiction using

Corollary
D.28
.
∎

D.4.1.
Type Safety Revisited

We are finally ready to prove type safety of
F^{\omega}_{\displaystyle\cdot\cdot}
.
The proof of weak preservation requires a standard generation lemma
for term and type abstractions.

Lemma D.32 (generation of typing for term and type abstraction).

(1)

If

\;\Gamma\vdash\lambda{x}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{A}.\,t:B
, then

\Gamma,x\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muA\vdash t:C
and

\,\Gamma\vdash A\rightarrow C\leq B:{*}
for some
C
.

(2)

If

\;\Gamma\vdash\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,t:A
, then

\Gamma,X\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muK\vdash t:B
and

\,\Gamma\vdash\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{B}\leq A:{*}
for some
B
.

Recall that weak preservation (
Prop.
3.2
) states that
CBV reduction preserves the types of closed terms, i.e. if

\vdash t:A
and
t\longrightarrow_{\sf v}t^{\prime}
, then
\vdash t^{\prime}:A
.

Proof of
Prop.
3.2
.

The proof is by induction on typing derivations and case analysis on
CBV reduction rules.
The interesting cases are those where
\beta
-contractions occur.
We describe the case of
T-App
.
The corresponding case for
T-TApp
is similar.
We have
t={(\lambda{x}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{B}.\,s)}\,{v}
with

\vdash\lambda{x}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{B}.\,s:C\rightarrow A
and
\vdash v:C
for some
B

and
C
.
By generation of term abstractions
(
Lemma
D.32
.
1
),

x\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muB\vdash s:D
and

\vdash B\rightarrow D\leq C\rightarrow A:{*}
for some
D
.
By inversion of subtyping (
Lemma
D.29
.
2
), we have

\vdash C\leq B:{*}
and
\vdash D\leq A:{*}
and
hence
\vdash v:B
and
x\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0muB\vdash s:A
by subsumption.
To conclude the proof we need to show that

\vdash s[v/x]:A
, which follows from the substitution
lemma (
Lemma
B.4
).
∎

This establishes the first half of type safety. For the second half,
progress, we first need to prove a standard canonical forms lemma.

Lemma D.33 (canonical forms).

Let
v
be a closed, well-typed value.

(1)

If
\;\varnothing\vdash v\in A\rightarrow B
, then

v=\lambda{x}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{C}.\,t
for some
C
and
t
.

(2)

If
\;\varnothing\vdash v\in\forall{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{K}.\,{A}
,
then
v=\lambda{X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J}.\,t
for some
J
and
t
.

Proof.

Separately for the two parts; each by case analysis, first on
v
,
then on the final typing rule used to derive the respective premise.
Since the only values are abstractions, the relevant sub-cases
are
T-Abs
,
T-TAbs
and
T-Sub
. The
sub-cases for
T-Abs
and
T-TAbs
are immediate.
In the sub-cases for
T-Sub
, we first use the generation
lemma for abstractions (
Lemma
D.32
), then dismiss
impossible sub-cases using
Lemma
D.31
.
∎

Thanks to the canonical forms lemma, the proof of the progress theorem
is now entirely standard.

Theorem D.34 (progress).

If
\;\vdash t:A
, then either
t
is a value, or
t\longrightarrow_{\sf v}t^{\prime}

for some term
t^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By routine induction on typing derivations. The cases
for
T-App
and
T-TApp
use the canonical forms
lemma (
Lemma
D.33
).
∎

Appendix E
Encoding Custom Subtyping Theories

Disclaimer.
The following examples have not been
mechanized in Agda.

Recall that bindings of the form
X:A\intv B
represent
first-class
type inequations

A\leq B
in
F^{\omega}_{\displaystyle\cdot\cdot}

because, in a context containing such a binding, we have
A\leq X

and
X\leq B
, and hence – by transitivity of subtyping –

A\leq B
.
Interval kinds thus provide us with a mechanism for
(in)equality
reflection
, i.e. a way to extend the subtyping relation via
assumptions made at the term- or type-level (via type abstractions).
Among other things, this allows us to postulate type operators with
associated subtyping rules through type variable bindings.
For example, we may postulate intersection types
A\wedge B
by
assuming an abstract binary type operator

\bar{\wedge}:{*}\rightarrow{*}\rightarrow{*}
and the usual typing
rules for intersections as abstract type inequations:

\displaystyle Y_{\leq L}:({X_{1},X_{2}}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{{*}})\rightarrow{({{\bar{\wedge}}\,{X_{1}}}\,{X_{2}})\intv X_{1}},\qquad Y_{\leq R}:({X_{1},X_{2}}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{{*}})\rightarrow{({{\bar{\wedge}}\,{X_{1}}}\,{X_{2}})\intv X_{2}},

\displaystyle Y_{\leq\wedge}:({Z,X_{1},X_{2}}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{{*}})\rightarrow{Z\intv X_{1}\rightarrow Z\intv X_{2}\rightarrow Z\intv\mskip 1.0mu{}({{\bar{\wedge}}\,{X_{1}}}\,{X_{2}})}.

where we abbreviated dependent arrow kinds

({X_{1}}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{({X_{2}}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{\dotsm\rightarrow({X_{n}}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{K}}}
with
multiple parameters
as
({X_{1},X_{2},\dotsc,X_{n}}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{J})\rightarrow{K}
for readability.
The two variables
Y_{\leq L}
and
Y_{\leq R}
represent,
respectively, the left- and right-hand projection rules for
intersections (
A\wedge B\leq A
and
A\wedge B\leq B
).
The assumption
Y_{\leq\wedge}
encodes the fact that
intersections are greatest lower bounds, i.e. that

A\leq B\wedge C

when
A\leq B
and
A\leq C
.
To see how this last “rule” can be put to work, let
A
,
B
and
C

be proper types and assume that
A\leq B
and
A\leq C
. Then we
also have
A:A\intv B
and
A:A\intv C
, and hence

{{{{{Y_{\leq\wedge}}\,{A}}\,{B}}\,{C}}\,{A}}\,{A}:A\intv\mskip 1.0mu{}({{\bar{\wedge}}\,{B}}\,{C})
, from which we conclude

A\leq{{\bar{\wedge}}\,{B}}\,{C}
.

We can also postulate recursive inequations. For example, the
following bindings encode an equi-recursive type constructor

\mu:({*}\rightarrow{*})\rightarrow{*}
that, when applied to
a unary operator
A
, represents the fixpoint
{\mu}\,{A}
of
A
.

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}:({*}\rightarrow{*})\rightarrow{*},

\displaystyle Y_{\leq L}:({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{{*}\rightarrow{*}})\rightarrow{({\bar{\mu}}\,{X})\intv\mskip 1.0mu{}({X}\,{({\bar{\mu}}\,{X})})},

\displaystyle Y_{\leq R}:({X}\mskip 1.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{{*}\rightarrow{*}})\rightarrow{({X}\,{({\bar{\mu}}\,{X})})\intv\mskip 1.0mu{}({\bar{\mu}}\,{X})}.

Together, the assumptions
Y_{\leq L}
and
Y_{\leq R}
say
that
{\mu}\,{A}={A}\,{({\mu}\,{A})}
, i.e. that

{\mu}\,{A}
is a fixpoint of
A
.

The above examples are only possible because we do not impose any

consistency constraints
on the bounds of intervals.
That is, an interval kind
A\intv B
is well-formed, irrespective of
whether
A\leq B
is actually provable or not.
For example, the signature of the abstract intersection operator

\bar{\wedge}
above does not tell us anything about how the type
application
{{\bar{\wedge}}\,{A}}\,{B}
is related to its first argument

A
, nor does the abstract left projection inequality
Y_{\leq L}

impose any constraints on its parameters
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
.
It is therefore impossible to say anything about the relationship of
the bounds
{{\bar{\wedge}}\,{X_{1}}}\,{X_{2}}
and
X_{1}
of the codomain of

Y_{\leq L}
, other than that they are both proper types.
We can certainly not prove that
{{\bar{\wedge}}\,{X_{1}}}\,{X_{2}}\leq X_{1}

in general.
